Greek (Kühn)

καὶ χρὴ τὸν μέλλοντα τοῖς ἐν αὐτῷ λεγομένοις ἀκολουθήσειν ὡμιληκέναι τῷ δευτέρῳ πρότερον ἐπιμελῶς, ἐν ᾧ πρῶτον μὲν τὴν ὅλην γνώμην ἐδείξαμεν τοῦ Ἱπποκράτους, ὅτι μηδὲν τῶν ἐπιτυχόντων, μηδ’ ὧν οἱ πολλοὶ τῶν ἰατρῶν ἴσασι, γράφειν ἀξιοῖ, δεύτερον δὲ καὶ κατὰ μέρος ἅπαντας ἐπήλθομεν τοὺς ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ τε καὶ τρίτῳ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν ἀῤῥώστους, ἐπιδεικνύντες ἐπὶ πολλῶν μὲν, ὅτι εἰ καὶ δυσπνοεῖν ἀναγκαῖον ὂν τοῖς κάμνουσιν, ὅμως οὐκ ἔγραψε, καθάπερ οὐδ’ ἄλλα 890 πολλὰ τῶν ἐξ ἀνάγκης τοῖς γεγραμμένοις ἑπομένων, ἃ γὰρ ἔστι καὶ χωρὶς τοῦ παρ’ ἐκείνου μαθεῖν, ἐξ ὧν εἶπεν ὁρμώμενον αὐτόν τινα ἐξευρεῖν, ταῦτ’ οὐκ ᾤετο χρῆναι γράφειν, ἐπὶ πολλῶν δὲ καὶ τὸ τῆς δυσπνοίας εἶδος αὐταῖς παραγράφει ταῖς τῶν νοσούντων διηγήσεσιν, ἐπειδὰν ἤτοι μὴ κατ’ ἀνάγκην ἕπηται τοῖς νοσοῦσιν, ἢ μὴ πρόχειρον ᾖ γνωσθῆναι τοῖς ἀναλεγομένοις αὐτοῦ τὰ γράμματα. τούτων οὖν ἐχόμενοι καὶ νῦν τῶν σκοπῶν, ἐπειδὴ τὸ πρῶτόν τε καὶ τὸ τρίτον τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν διὰ τῶν ἔμπροσθεν ἐξηγησάμεθα, πάλιν ἀπὸ τοῦ δευτέρου τὴν ἀρχὴν ποιησόμεθα, συνάπτοντες αὐτῷ καὶ τὰ λοιπὰ τῶν Ἱπποκράτους συγγραμμάτων. ὥσπερ δὲ τὸ πρῶτον καὶ τὸ τρίτον οὐ μόνον ὑφ’ Ἱπποκράτους συγκεῖσθαι δοκεῖ τοῖς ἄριστα περὶ τούτων ἐγνωκόσιν, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῇ τῆς θεωρίας οἰκειότητι συνῆφθαι πρὸς ἄλληλα, τὸν αὐτὸν οἶμαι τρόπον ἔχειν καὶ τὰ κατὰ τὸ δεύτερον καὶ τὸ δ΄ καὶ τὸ ἕκτον. ὡμολόγηται γὰρ οὖν ἤδη καὶ ταῦτα συνθεῖναι Θεσσαλὸν τὸν Ἱπποκράτους υἱὸν, τὰ μὲν αὐτοῦ τοῦ πατρὸς ἐν διφθέραις τισὶν ἢ δέλτοις εὑρόντα ὑπομνήματα, προσθέντα δέ τινα καὶ αὐτὸν οὐκ ὀλίγα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀναγκαῖον ὂν

And anyone who intends to follow what is said in it must first have studied the second carefully, in which we showed, first, the whole purpose of Hippocrates, namely that he does not think fit to write down anything of what is commonplace, nor of what the majority of physicians know; and second, we also went through in detail all the patients in the first and third books of the Epidemics, demonstrating in the case of many of them that, even though it was necessary for the sufferers to breathe with difficulty, nevertheless he did not write it down, just as he also did not write many 890 other things which follow of necessity from what has been written—for the things which it is possible to discover even without learning them from him, if one sets out from what he has said, these he did not think it necessary to write—while in the case of many he also adds the form of the dyspnoea to the very narratives of the sick, whenever either it does not follow of necessity for the sick, or it is not readily apparent to those who read through his writings. Holding, then, to these aims now too, since we have expounded the first and the third books of the Epidemics in what precedes, we shall again make our beginning from the second, joining to it also the remaining writings of Hippocrates. And just as the first and the third are thought, by those who have judged best about these matters, not only to have been composed by Hippocrates, but also to be connected with one another by the affinity of their theory, in the same way, I think, it stands also with the contents of the second and the fourth and the sixth. For it is indeed already agreed that these too were put together by Thessalus the son of Hippocrates, who found the notes of his father himself on certain parchments or tablets, and also added not a few things himself.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τὸ δ΄ (the numeral reads 'fourth', but the following passage denies that the fourth book is by Thessalus; the text may be corrupt), διφθέραις (rendered 'skins'; possibly 'parchments'), δέλτοις

And whoever is going to follow what is said in it must first have carefully studied the second book. In that book we first showed the whole intention of Hippocrates: that he does not think fit to write anything commonplace, nor anything that most physicians know. Second, we went through one by one all the patients in the first and third books of the Epidemics. We showed in many cases that, even though the patients must necessarily have had difficulty breathing, he nevertheless did not write it down, just as he did not write down many other things 890 that follow of necessity from what he has written. For what one can learn even without him, finding it out for oneself by starting from what he said, he did not think needed to be written. But in many cases he also adds the form of the difficulty of breathing to the very narratives of the sick, whenever either it does not follow of necessity in the sick, or it is not readily recognisable to those who read his writings. Holding to these aims now as well, since we explained the first and third books of the Epidemics in what preceded, we shall again make our beginning from the second, joining to it the remaining writings of Hippocrates as well. Those who know these matters best think that the first and third books were not only composed by Hippocrates but also joined to one another by the affinity of their theory. Just so, I think, the same holds for the contents of the second, the fourth and the sixth. For it is in fact already agreed that these too were put together by Thessalus, the son of Hippocrates, who found notes of his father himself on certain skins or writing-tablets, and himself added not a few things as well.

Greek (Kühn)

τό γε 891 μὴν πέμπτον τε καὶ ἕβδομον τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν οὐκ ἄν τίς μοι δοκεῖ τῆς Ἱπποκράτους γνώμης οἰκείας ἀξιῶσαι, κατ’ ἐμὴν δὲ γνώμην οὐδὲ τὸ τέταρτον, εἰ καί τισιν ἔδοξε καὶ τοῦθ’ ὑπὸ Θεσσαλοῦ συντεθῆναι· τὸ δὲ προγνωστικὸν καὶ τοὺς ἀφορισμοὺς καὶ τὸ περὶ ὑδάτων καὶ τόπων καὶ ἀέρων, ἔτι τε τὸ περὶ διαίτης ὀξέων, ὅ τινες ἐπιγράφουσι πρὸς τὰς Κνιδίας γνώμας, οὐκ ἀλόγως Ἱπποκράτους αὐτοῦ πεπίστευται γράμματα εἶναι. τούτων τε οὖν τῶν βιβλίων ἔνθ’ ἂν ἡ χρεία καλῇ μνημονεύσομεν, ὅσα τ’ ἄλλα τὰ μὲν εἰς αὐτὸν Ἱπποκράτην, τὰ δὲ εἰς Εὐρυφῶντα καὶ Θεσσαλὸν καὶ Πόλυβον ἀναφέρουσιν. Ἀρκτέον οὖν ἤδη τῆς ἐξηγήσεως ἀπὸ τῆς ἐν τῷ δευτέρῳ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν γεγραμμένης ῥήσεως, ἐχούσης ὧδε· πνεῦμα σμικρὸν, πυκνόν· μέγα, ἀραιόν· σμικρὸν, ἀραιόν· ἔξω μέγα, εἴσω μικρόν· τὸ μὲν ἐκτεῖνον, τὸ δὲ κατεπεῖγον. διπλῆ ἔσω ἐπανάκλησις, οἷον ἐπεισπνέουσιν· θερμὸν, ψυχρόν. ἡ δ’ αὐτὴ ῥῆσις ἥδε ἐν τῷ ἕκτῳ γέγραπται τελεώτερον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπανάκλησις, ἐπεισπνέουσιν

The fifth and 891 seventh books of the Epidemics, however, no one, it seems to me, would judge worthy of being proper to the thought of Hippocrates; and in my own opinion not the fourth either, even if some have thought that this too was composed by Thessalus. But the Prognostic, the Aphorisms, On Waters, Places and Airs, and further On Regimen in Acute Diseases, which some entitle Against the Cnidian Sentences, are not unreasonably believed to be writings of Hippocrates himself. We shall therefore make mention of these books wherever need calls for it, and of all the others which they refer, some to Hippocrates himself, others to Euryphon, Thessalus and Polybus. We must now begin the exposition from the passage written in the second book of the Epidemics, which runs as follows: breath small, dense; large, rare; small, rare; large outward, small inward; the one drawn out, the other hurried. A double inward recall (ἐπανάκλησις), as in those who breathe in additionally; hot, cold. And this same passage is written more completely in the sixth book.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπανάκλησις, ἐπεισπνέουσιν, κατεπεῖγον, ἐκτεῖνον, οὐκ ἄν τίς μοι δοκεῖ ... ἀξιῶσαι

The 891 fifth and seventh books of the Epidemics, however, no one, it seems to me, would deem to belong to Hippocrates' own thought; nor, in my opinion, the fourth either, even if some have thought that this too was composed by Thessalus. But the Prognostic and the Aphorisms and the work On Waters, Places and Airs, and further the work On Regimen in Acute Diseases, which some entitle Against the Cnidian Opinions, are not without reason believed to be writings of Hippocrates himself. We shall therefore mention these books wherever need calls for it, together with all the others that people ascribe, some to Hippocrates himself, others to Euryphon, Thessalus and Polybus. We must, then, now begin the exegesis from the passage written in the second book of the Epidemics, which runs thus: 'Breath small, frequent; large, sparse; small, sparse; outward large, inward small; the one extending, the other hastening. A double inward recall, as if they breathe in on top; hot, cold.' This same passage is written more completely in the sixth book.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπὶ γὰρ ταῖς τρίταις συζυγίαις αὐτῆς δυσπνοίας ἡ τετάρτη πρόσκειται, 892 παραλελειμμένη σαφῶς ἐν τῷ δευτέρῳ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν, οὐχ ὑπὸ τοῦ γράψαντος, ἐμοὶ δοκεῖν, αὐτοῦ· δῆλος γάρ ἐστιν οὐκ ἐξ ἐμπειρίας τινὸς καὶ μνήμης ἀθροίζων τὸ πλῆθος τῶν δυσπνοιῶν, ἀλλ’ ἐκ λογικῆς διαιρέσεως· οὐκ οὖν ἔμελλε παραλείψειν τινὰ δύσπνοιαν· ἀλλά τις τῶν παλαιῶν βιβλιογράφων ἥμαρτεν, ἔπειθ’ οὕτως ἔχον ἔφθασεν ἐκδοθῆναι τὸ βιβλίον, ἐφυλάχθη τε εἰκότως μέχρι δεῦρο τοῦτ’ αὐτὸ τὸ σφάλμα, τινῶν μὲν ὀλιγώρως ὁμιλούντων τοῖς τῶν παλαιῶν βιβλίοις, ὡς μήτ’ εἰ λείπει τι, μήτ’ εἰ δι’ ἑτέρου γράμματος εἴρηται, γνωρίζειν, ἐνίων δὲ γνωριζόντων μὲν, ἀλλὰ προσθεῖναι τὸ λεῖπον οὐ τολμώντων. οὐ γὰρ δὴ ὁμοίως τοῖς νῦν οἱ πρόσθεν εὐχερεῖς ἦσαν οὐδ’ ἕτοιμοι παραγράφειν παλαιὰν λέξιν ὡσαύτως γεγραμμένην ἐν ἅπασι τοῖς ἀντιγράφοις, ἀλλ’ ἐπισημήνασθαι μόνον αὐτοῖς ἱκανὸν ἦν, ὡς ἐνταῦθα μὲν ἐλλιπῶς, ἐν τῷ ἕκτῳ δὲ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν τελέως ἡ διαίρεσις γέγραπται τῶν δυσπνοιῶν, ἥ τε κατὰ μέγεθος καὶ μικρότητα καὶ πυκνότητα καὶ ἀραιότητα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τρίταις, αὐτῆς, εὐχερεῖς, παραγράφειν

For to the three conjugations of dyspnoea itself the fourth is added there, 892 which has clearly been omitted in the second book of the Epidemics — not, in my opinion, by the writer himself; for he is evidently assembling the full number of the dyspnoeas not from some experience and memory, but from logical division; he was not, therefore, going to omit any dyspnoea. Rather, one of the ancient copyists erred; then the book, being in this condition, had already been published, and this very error was understandably preserved down to the present day, since some people engage carelessly with the books of the ancients, so as to recognise neither whether something is missing nor whether it has been expressed with a different letter, while some do recognise it but do not dare to add what is missing. For those of former times were not, like those of today, cavalier, nor ready to alter an ancient reading written in the same way in all the copies; rather, it was enough for them merely to note that here the division of the dyspnoeas is written incompletely, but in the sixth book of the Epidemics completely — the division according to greatness and smallness and frequency and rarity.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τρίταις (the sense requires 'three', perhaps for τρισί), αὐτῆς (perhaps for αὐτῇ, 'in it', i.e. in the passage of Epidemics VI), ἔφθασεν ἐκδοθῆναι, δι’ ἑτέρου γράμματος, εὐχερεῖς, παραγράφειν

For in it, to the three combinations of difficulty of breathing the fourth is added, 892 which has clearly been omitted in the second book of the Epidemics, though not, in my opinion, by the writer himself. For he is plainly gathering the number of the difficulties of breathing not from some experience and memory but from logical division, and so he was not going to omit any difficulty of breathing. Rather, one of the ancient copyists made a mistake, and then the book, being in this state, was published before the mistake was noticed, and this very error has, understandably, been preserved down to the present. Some people engage carelessly with the books of the ancients, so that they recognise neither whether something is missing nor whether it has been written with a different letter, while others do recognise it but do not dare to add what is missing. For the people of earlier times were not, like those of today, rash or ready to alter an ancient reading written in the same way in all the copies. It was enough for them merely to note that here the division of the difficulties of breathing is written incompletely, but completely in the sixth book of the Epidemics, namely the division according to largeness and smallness and frequency and infrequency.

K7.892-893K. VII 892–893, ch. 3.2 difficulty low (1.29/100; thin 1, absent 1) (a) missing (b) fail 4/8 (c) judge_failed_gate unit: mixed

Greek (Kühn)

δύο γὰρ οὖν δὴ ταύτας ἀντιθέσεις ὁ Ἱπποκράτης, τήν τε κατὰ μέγεθος ﻿ 893 καὶ μικρότητα καὶ τὴν ἀραιότητα καὶ πυκνότητα, δῆλός ἐστιν ἐπιπλέκων ἀλλήλαις, καὶ ποιῶν συζυγίας δυσπνοίας δ΄· μίαν μὲν ἐν ᾗ μικροτέρα τε ἅμα καὶ πυκνοτέρα τῆς κατὰ φύσιν ἡ ἀναπνοὴ γίγνεται, ἑτέραν δ’ ὅταν μείζων τε ἅμα καὶ ἀραιοτέρα, καὶ πρὸς ταύταις ἄλλην τρίτην ἐν ᾗ μικροτέρα τε καὶ ἀραιοτέρα, καὶ πρὸς ταύταις τετάρτην, ἣν ἐν τῷ δευτέρῳ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν παραλελεῖφθαί φαμεν, ἐν ᾗ μείζων τε ἅμα καὶ πυκνοτέρα γίγνεται. δέδεικται γάρ μοι πολλάκις, εἰ καὶ ἄρα χωρὶς τοῦ δειχθῆναι πρόδηλον ὑπάρχει πᾶσιν, ὡς δύο ἀντιθέσεις ἀλλήλαις ἐπιπλεκόμεναι, τέσσαρας ἀπεργάζονται συζυγίας. οὐ μόνον δὲ κατ’ αὐτὸ διενήνοχεν ἡ ἐν τῷ ἕκτῳ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν ῥῆσις τῆς ἐν τῷ δευτέρῳ, ἀλλὰ καὶ καθ’ ὅσον ἐν τοῖς ἐφεξῆς, ὁπότε παραβάλλει τὸ τῆς εἰσπνοῆς μέγεθος τῷ τῆς ἐκπνοῆς, ἡ ἑτέρα συζυγία παραλέλειπται παντάπασιν ἐν τῷ δευτέρῳ, ἐνδεικνυμένου κᾀνταῦθα σαφῶς τοῦ γραφέως ἤτοι ῥᾳθυμίαν ἐσχάτην, ἢ ῥᾳδιουργίαν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ῥᾳδιουργίαν

For Hippocrates is clearly interweaving these two oppositions with one another, the one according to greatness 893 and smallness and the one of rarity and frequency, and making four conjugations of dyspnoea: one in which the respiration becomes at once smaller and more frequent than that according to nature; another when it becomes at once greater and rarer; and in addition to these a third, in which it is smaller and rarer; and in addition to these a fourth, which we say has been omitted in the second book of the Epidemics, in which it becomes at once greater and more frequent. For it has been shown by me many times—even if indeed it is obvious to everyone without being shown—that two oppositions interwoven with one another produce four conjugations. And the passage in the sixth book of the Epidemics differs from the one in the second not only in this respect, but also insofar as, in what follows, when he compares the greatness of the inspiration with that of the expiration, the other conjugation has been altogether omitted in the second, the copyist here too clearly displaying either extreme negligence or rash tampering.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ῥᾳδιουργίαν, ἡ ἑτέρα συζυγία

For indeed it is evident that Hippocrates combines these two antitheses with each other, that according to largeness 893 and smallness and that of infrequency and frequency, and so makes four pairings of difficulty of breathing: one in which the breathing becomes at once smaller and more frequent than the natural; another when it is at once larger and more infrequent; and besides these a third, in which it is smaller and more infrequent; and besides these a fourth, which we say has been omitted in the second book of the Epidemics, in which it becomes at once larger and more frequent. For I have shown many times, even if it is in fact plain to everyone even without being shown, that two antitheses combined with one another produce four pairings. And the passage in the sixth book of the Epidemics differs from that in the second not only in this very respect, but also inasmuch as in what follows, where he compares the magnitude of inspiration with that of expiration, one of the two pairings has been entirely omitted in the second book; here too the copyist clearly displays either the utmost indolence or unscrupulous tampering.

For if he skipped over whole phrases, he was careless in the extreme; but if, not knowing in what respect phrases that resemble one another differ, he supposed that both indicated one and the same thing, and then 894 removed the one of them, he had reached the very summit of officiousness, declaring himself a corrector of Hippocrates' writings, not a servant of them. This, indeed, it is useful to bear in mind with regard to many of the obscure passages, in which it is plausible that something has either been omitted or been miswritten. For nothing prevents us, in those passages, either by supplying what is lacking or by correcting what is erroneous, from rendering the statement complete and true. For just as rewriting the ancient passages is rash, so too, while preserving them as they are written, to resolve the difficulties by certain brief additions or alterations is the task of good exegetes. But concerning these matters I think I shall show a little later, through the actual practice, what one ought to do. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For that 895 he attempts to discover all the differences of dyspnoeas from a logical division, this both seems so to those who have expounded his books, and I think it is altogether easy to infer; but whether he divides correctly, and whether he omits no kind of dyspnoea, this is no longer easy for everyone to discover; rather, the one who is to judge concerning these matters must be experienced in the method of division, and not unpractised in the doctrines of Hippocrates. Now the method says that one must look to the thing being divided, what sort of thing it is in its nature. For if it should be simple, one must examine the things that belong to it according to the account of its essence, how many they are and which and of what sort, and divide on the basis of these; but if it is composite, one must divide each of the two, or each of the parts, as has been said, and the whole on the basis of the manner of its composition.

The opinion of Hippocrates about the composition, whatever it may be, has been stated by us at very great length elsewhere, and has been stated also in what precedes, so that nothing prevents us, looking to these aims, from examining the passage of the Epidemics, which runs somewhat as follows: breaths small and frequent, large and rare; small and rare, 896 large and frequent; large outward, small inward; large inward, small outward; the one drawn out, the other hurried. A double inward recall, as in those who breathe in upon a breath; hot, cold. That, then, not only the fourth conjugation, that according to frequent and large, has been omitted in the second book of the Epidemics, but also that, of the two that follow, in which he compares expiration with inspiration, the second of these is written in the passage now set before us but has been omitted in the second book of the Epidemics, is easy to see. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διαστάσεων

That, then, the activity of respiration as a whole is composite, made up of the inward and the outward movement of the breath, and that one of its parts is called inspiration and the other expiration, 897 has been stated in the first book right at the beginning; but even if it had not been said by us, there is hardly anyone who does not know it. And that speed and slowness are proper to nothing else among all things than motion, this too has already been said before, but let it be recalled now as well. Largeness and smallness, however, just as they are not proper to motion, so they are inseparable from everything that moves over an interval. And for this reason, in each of the two parts of respiration there will be two oppositions of differences, one according to speed and slowness, and a second according to largeness and smallness. And that of respiration as a whole there is a further opposition, constituted according to frequency and rarity, this too has been said before, as also that this opposition belongs to respiration not as a motion but as a composite thing, being made up of intervals (διαστάσεων).

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κατὰ διαστήματος, ἐκ διαστάσεών

That the activity of the whole respiration is compounded of the inward and the outward movement of the breath, and that one of its parts is called inspiration and the other expiration, 897 has been stated already in the first book, right at the beginning; but even if it had not been stated by us, hardly anyone is unaware of it. That speed and slowness are proper to nothing else of all things than to motion, this too has been stated before, but let it now also be called back to memory. Magnitude and smallness, however, while they are not proper to motion, are nonetheless inseparable from everything that moves across an extent. And for this reason, in each of the parts of respiration there will arise two oppositions of differences: one according to speed and slowness, the second according to magnitude and smallness. As for the whole respiration, that there is a further opposition constituted according to frequency and infrequency, this too has been stated before, and also that this opposition is composed of intervals, not as belonging to respiration as a motion, but as belonging to a compound thing.

ὥστ’ ἐν τρισὶ τούτοις γένεσιν ἀνάγκη πάσας εἶναι τὰς τῆς ἀναπνοῆς διαφοράς· ἐν τάχει μὲν καὶ βραδυτῆτι, διότι κινήσεως ἁπάσης ἴδιά τε καὶ ἀχώριστα ταῦτ’ ἐστίν· ἐν μεγέθει δὲ καὶ μικρότητι, διότι κατὰ διαστήματος ἡ τῶν ἀναπνευστικῶν ὀργάνων κίνησις· πυκνότης ﻿ 898 δὲ καὶ ἀραιότης, τὸ τρίτον τῶν εἰρημένων γενῶν, οὐδετέρου μέν ἐστι τῶν οἰκείων τε καὶ πρώτων μερῶν τῆς ἀναπνοῆς οὔτ’ ἴδιον οὔτ’ ἀχώριστον, ὅλης δὲ μέρος γίνεται κατά τι συμβεβηκὸς, ἐπειδὴ σύνθετον πρᾶγμά ἐστιν ἐκ δυοῖν ἐναντίων κινήσεων ἡσυχίᾳ διαιρουμένων. αὗται μὲν οὖν αἱ πρῶται διαφοραὶ τῆς ὅλης ἀναπνοῆς. τῷ δ’ εἶναι ποιεῖν τι καὶ πάσχειν πεφυκὸς τὴν ἀναπνοὴν, εἴη ἄν τι καὶ ἄλλο γένος ἐν αὐτῷ διαφορῶν, ἑκατέρου τῶν μορίων ὁμαλῶς ἢ ἀνωμάλως ἐπιτελουμένου· καὶ μὴν καὶ ἄλλο πάλιν ὅλης αὐτῆς ὡς συνθέτου, παραβαλλομένων ἀλλήλοις τῶν μορίων. τούτων οὕτως ἐχόντων, ἰστέον τὰς μὲν πρώτας γεγραμμένας ἐν τῇ προκειμένῃ ῥήσει δυσπνοιῶν διαφορὰς τέτταρας ὅλης ἀναπνοῆς ὑπάρχειν· οὐδετέρου γὰρ τῶν μερῶν ἐστι πυκνότης καὶ ἀραιότης, εἴγε δὴ μεμνήμεθα τῶν εὐθὺς κατ’ ἀρχὰς τοῦ πρώτου τῶνδε τῶν ὑπομνημάτων εἰρημένων.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μέρος γίνεται κατά τι συμβεβηκὸς

So that all the differences of respiration must necessarily fall within these three genera: in speed and slowness, because these are proper to and inseparable from every motion; in largeness and smallness, because the motion of the respiratory organs is a motion over an interval; while density 898 and rarity, the third of the genera mentioned, is neither proper to nor inseparable from either of the proper and primary parts of respiration, but comes to belong to the whole as a part by a certain accident, since respiration is a composite thing made up of two contrary motions separated by rest. These, then, are the primary differences of the whole respiration. But because respiration is of a nature to act in some way and to be acted upon, there would also be another genus of differences in it, according as each of the two parts is accomplished evenly or unevenly; and indeed yet another again, belonging to the whole of it as composite, when the parts are compared with one another. This being so, one must know that the first four differences of dyspnoeas written down in the passage before us belong to the whole respiration; for density and rarity belong to neither of the parts, if indeed we remember what was said right at the beginning of the first of these commentaries.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τῷ δ’ εἶναι ποιεῖν τι καὶ πάσχειν πεφυκὸς, ὅλης δὲ μέρος γίνεται, κατὰ διαστήματος

So it is necessary that all the differences of breathing lie in these three genera: in speed and slowness, because these are proper to, and inseparable from, every motion; in largeness and smallness, because the motion of the respiratory organs proceeds through an interval; frequency 898 and infrequency, the third of the genera mentioned, is neither proper to nor inseparable from either of the proper and primary parts of breathing, but becomes a part of breathing as a whole by a kind of accident, since breathing is a compound thing made up of two opposite motions divided by rest. These, then, are the primary differences of breathing as a whole. But because breathing is by nature such as to act and to be acted upon, there would also be another genus of differences in it, according as each of the two parts is carried out evenly or unevenly; and moreover yet another genus belonging to breathing as a whole, as a compound, when the parts are compared with one another. This being so, one must know that the first four differences of difficulties of breathing written in the passage before us belong to breathing as a whole; for frequency and infrequency belong to neither of the parts, if indeed we remember what was said right at the beginning of the first of these commentaries.

And so too the two that follow them; for in these too the two parts of the whole respiration are compared with one another, and for this reason it has been written thus: "outward large, inward small; inward large, outward small" — breath, evidently, being understood. But the two differences that come next, 899 in which the one is called the "extended" (ἐκτεῖνον) and the other the "hurried" (κατεπεῖγον), must be considered to be proper to either one of the parts of respiration — that which, indeed, we said belongs most of all to the substance of respiration; for the "extended" is the slow, and the "hurried" is the rapid. And slowness and rapidity are inseparable from every motion.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐκτεῖνον, κατεπεῖγον

And so too the two that follow them; for in these also the two parts of the whole respiration are compared with each other, and for this reason it has been written thus: 'outwards large, inwards small; inwards large, outwards small', meaning, evidently, the breath (pneuma). The two differences that follow next, 899 in which it is said 'the one drawn out, the other hurried', must be held to be peculiar to one of the two parts of respiration, the one which we said belongs most of all to the essence of respiration; for 'drawn out' is the slow, and 'hurried' the fast. And slowness and speed are inseparable from every motion.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπεισπνέουσιν, θερμῷ ψυχρόν

What is said next—'double recall inward, as in those who breathe in additionally (ἐπεισπνέουσιν)'—is indeed indicative of uneven inspiration, but it demands the same antithesis in the case of expiration, which I am persuaded was omitted by the copyists who transcribed the text rather than by Hippocrates himself, if indeed he plainly mentions in other writings this kind of dyspnoea as well, as I shall show clearly in what follows. If, however, anyone thinks it was omitted by Hippocrates himself, that person chooses the worse over the better. For not to have mentioned unevenness at all in either of the two parts of respiration, but to have completely forgotten and omitted it—not even this would have been in keeping with the carefulness of Hippocrates; rather, for my part, even if what is now written about inspiration had not been written at all, 900 I would have ventured to say that this was a fault of the copyist rather than of Hippocrates—though in that case the view would at least have had some rationale for those who take the worse interpretation. But since he indicated the form of the inspiration so clearly that he not only said that the recall of the breath inward becomes double, but also added the phrase 'as in those who breathe in additionally', how could anyone still suppose that he forgot expiration? About these matters, then, more again later; but next there is added another antithesis of dyspnoeas, written at the end of the whole passage roughly as follows: 'hot, cold'—so that here too, evidently, we are to supply 'breath'. For just as the breath is breathed out great and small, and frequent and rare, so too it is breathed out hot and cold. And yet here too some, misunderstanding on the basis of what they themselves do not comprehend, 'correct' and venture to rewrite the wording, writing not 'cold and hot', as it has now been stated, but joining these words to what precedes and reading thus: 'as in those who breathe in cold upon hot' (οἷον ἐπεισπνέουσι θερμῷ ψυχρόν).

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπεισπνέουσιν, λόγον δ’ ἂν οὖν εἶχεν οὕτω τινὰ, θερμῷ ψυχρόν

What is said next, 'a double recall inwards, as if they breathe in on top', is indicative of an uneven inspiration. It calls for the same antithesis in the case of expiration, and I am persuaded that this was left out by the copyists rather than by Hippocrates himself, since he clearly mentions this kind of dyspnoea too in other treatises, as I shall show plainly in what follows. If anyone thinks it was left out by Hippocrates himself, that person chooses the worse over the better. For to make no mention at all of unevenness in either of the parts of respiration, and to forget and leave it out entirely, would not have been in keeping with Hippocrates' carefulness either. Indeed, even if what is now written about inspiration had not been written at all, 900 I would have ventured to say that this was the copyist's error rather than Hippocrates'; yet it would still have had some plausibility for those who take the worse view. But since he indicated the form of inspiration so clearly, not only saying that a double recall of the breath inwards occurs but also adding 'as if they breathe in on top', how could anyone still suppose that he forgot about expiration? About these matters, then, more later. Next is added another antithesis of dyspnoeas, written at the end of the whole passage roughly thus: 'hot, cold', so that here too we evidently understand 'breath' in addition. For just as the breath is breathed out large and small, and frequent and sparse, so it is also breathed out hot and cold. And yet here too some, misunderstanding on the basis of things they themselves do not comprehend, make 'corrections' and venture to alter the wording. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

These men, then, in addition to making the text have no sense, 901 become manifest as not having even read the books of the ancient; for they would certainly have remembered what was written by him in other works about hot and cold expiration—and what reason he had either to write of these in those works or to omit them here, they would not be able to say. For if he did not consider hot and cold expiration to be among the dyspnoeas, he did not do well to mention them in other works as dyspnoeas; but if he acted rightly in those works, he did not rightly omit them now. But these matters do not stand thus: for both here they are rightly included in the division of the dyspnoeas, and what it is that they are of a nature to signify has been correctly stated in other works.

Καταλιπόντες οὖν ἤδη τοὺς οὕτω παρακούοντας τοῦ παλαιοῦ καὶ παντὸς μᾶλλον ἢ τῆς γνώμης αὐτοῦ συνιέντας, αὐτοὶ καθ’ ἡμᾶς αὐτοὺς ἐπισκεψώμεθα, τί δή ποτε κατὰ τὸ προγνωστικὸν γράμμα περὶ δυσπνοίας ὧδέ πως γράφει· πνεῦμα πυκνὸν μὲν ἐὸν πόνον σημαίνει ἢ φλεγμονὴν ἐν τοῖσιν ὑπὲρ τῶν φρενῶν χωρίοισι, μέγα δὲ ἀναπνεόμενον καὶ διὰ πολλοῦ χρόνου παραφροσύνην σημαίνει, ψυχρὸν δὲ ἐκπνεόμενον 902 ἐκ τῶν ῥινῶν καὶ τοῦ στόματος ὀλέθριον κάρτα ἤδη γίγνεται· φαίνεται γὰρ ἐν τούτῳ τῷ βιβλίῳ τριῶν τούτων μόνον δυσπνοιῶν μνημονεύων, καίτοι γ’ εἰ μὲν τοσαῦται μόνον εἰσὶν, τί δή ποτε πολὺ πλείους ἐν τοῖς τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν αὐτὸς ἔγραψε; εἰ δὲ οὕτως εἰσὶ πολλαὶ, τί δή ποτε ὀλιγίστων ἐμνημόνευσεν ἐν τῷ προγνωστικῷ; ἢ τὸν μὲν ἀριθμὸν αὐτῶν ἐπίστασθαι ἐνόμιζε χρηστὸν, ὁποῖον δέ τι δηλοῦν ἑκάστη πέφυκεν, οὐκ εἶναι. χρηστόν; ἢ μόναις ταῖς εἰρημέναις δηλοῦν ὑπάρχει τι, ταῖς δ’ ἄλλαις οὐδὲν, ἀλλ’ ἐνδέχεταί ποτ’ ἀναπνεῖν μὲν παρὰ φύσιν, οὐδεμίαν δ’ εἶναι διάθεσιν ἐν τῷ ζώῳ παρὰ φύσιν; ἢ τοσοῦτον δεῖ τοῦτ’ ἀληθὲς ὑπάρχειν, ὥστ’ ἀνάγκη πᾶσα τὴν παρὰ φύσιν ἐν τῷ ζώῳ διάθεσιν αὐτὴν γίγνεσθαι τῆς δυσπνοίας αἰτίαν; εἴπερ οὖν λόγον αἰτίας ἡ διάθεσις ἔχει πρὸς τὴν δύσπνοιαν, εἴη ἂν οὕτω καὶ χρόνῳ καὶ τάξει καὶ δυνάμει πρότερον. πῶς οὖν φαίνεται παραλιπὼν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης ἐν τῷ προγνωστικῷ τοσοῦτον ἀριθμὸν δυσπνοιῶν;

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Leaving aside, then, now those who thus misunderstand the ancient and comprehend anything rather than his meaning, let us ourselves, on our own account, examine why it is that in the Prognostic treatise he writes somewhat as follows about dyspnoea: "breath that is frequent signifies pain or inflammation in the regions above the diaphragm; breathed large and at long intervals it signifies delirium; and breathed out cold 902 from the nostrils and the mouth it is already exceedingly deadly." For in this book he appears to mention only these three dyspnoeas; and yet, if there are only so many, why ever did he himself write of many more in the books of the Epidemics? But if they are so many, why ever did he mention the fewest of them in the Prognostic? Or did he think it useful to know their number, but not useful to know what sort of thing each is of a nature to indicate? Or is it that only those mentioned indicate something, while the others indicate nothing, but it is possible at some time to breathe contrary to nature while there is no condition contrary to nature in the animal? Or rather, is this so far from being true that it is altogether necessary that the condition contrary to nature in the animal itself become the cause of the dyspnoea? If, then, the condition stands in the relation of cause to the dyspnoea, it would thus be prior both in time and in order and in power. How, then, does Hippocrates appear to have passed over so great a number of dyspnoeas in the Prognostic?

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὐκ εἶναι. χρηστόν;, παρακούοντας, ἀλλ’ ἐνδέχεταί

Leaving aside, then, those who so mishear the ancient and who understand anything rather than his meaning, let us ourselves consider by ourselves why on earth in the Prognostic treatise he writes about difficulty of breathing roughly as follows: 'Breath, when it is rapid, indicates pain or inflammation in the regions above the diaphragm; when it is drawn in large and at long intervals, it indicates delirium; and when it is breathed out cold 902 from the nostrils and the mouth, it is already exceedingly deadly.' For in this book he evidently mentions only these three difficulties of breathing. And yet, if there are only so many, why on earth did he himself write of far more in the books of the Epidemics? And if they are so many, why on earth did he mention very few in the Prognostic? Or did he think it useful to know their number, but not useful to know what each of them is of a nature to indicate? Or is it only the ones mentioned that indicate something, and the others nothing? Is it possible that one sometimes breathes contrary to nature, while there is no condition contrary to nature in the animal? Or is this so far from being true that it is altogether necessary that the condition contrary to nature in the animal itself becomes the cause of the difficulty of breathing? If, then, the condition stands in the relation of a cause to the difficulty of breathing, it would accordingly be prior in time, in order and in power. How, then, does Hippocrates evidently pass over so great a number of difficulties of breathing in the Prognostic?

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For these reasons I for my part wished both to compare the exegetes with one another 903 and to investigate and to resolve the matter; but the situation is not so. For of those who expound him at present, whom would anyone even mention? And as for the earlier ones, those at least whose commentaries survive down to our time, it is possible to find many and lengthy ravings written down, but matters as essential as these have been completely omitted. But let us at any rate investigate them ourselves, even if we have no path toward their discovery handed down to us by those men.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τίς ἄν τις καὶ μνημονεύοι

For these reasons I would have wished both to compare the commentators with one another 903 and to investigate and to resolve [the difficulty]; but the matter does not stand so. For of those who are commenting now, who would even make mention of any? And of those before them, at least those whose commentaries have been preserved down to our time, one can find many long pieces of nonsense set down in writing, while points so essential have been left out entirely. But let us at any rate investigate them ourselves, even if we have no path towards their discovery handed down by those men.

Greek (Kühn)

εἴρηται μὲν δὴ κᾀν τοῖς περὶ τῆς ἀρίστης αἱρέσεως κᾀν τοῖς τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου γράμμασιν, ὅπως χρὴ τὰ τοιαῦτα πάντα καὶ ζητεῖν καὶ ἀνευρίσκειν· ἀλλὰ κᾀν τῷ πρώτῳ τῶνδε τῶν ὑπομνημάτων διττὴν ὁδὸν ἔδειξα τῆς τῶν τοιούτων εὑρέσεως, ἑτέραν μὲν ἐκ τῶν ἐν τῇ δυσπνοίᾳ διαφορῶν ὁρμωμένην, ἑτέραν δ’ ἀπὸ τῶν ἐν τοῖς ἀναπνευστικοῖς ὀργάνοις διαθέσεων, ἣν καὶ ῥᾴω τε καὶ σαφεστέραν ὑπάρχειν ἔλεγον· καὶ ὅστις ἐκείνοις ἐπιμελῶς ἐνέτυχε τοῖς γράμμασιν, οὐ δεήσεται λόγων πολλῶν εἰς τὴν τῶν παρόντων λύσιν· εἰ δ’ ἤτοι ῥᾳθύμως ἐκείνοις ὡμίλησεν, ἢ ἄλλως ἐστὶ δυσμαθὴς, ὁ μὲν ῥᾴθυμος αὖθις (ἢ) ἀναλεξάμενος αὐτὰ πάλιν ἐπὶ ταῦθ’ ἡκέτω· ὁ σκαιὸς δ’ ἄλλό τι πραττέτω, γινώσκων 904 ὡς οὐχ οἵα τε μεγάλου γνώμην ἀνδρὸς ἐκμαθεῖν φύσις μικρὰ, ὁποῖος ἦν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης. ἃ γοῦν οὐδεὶς ἔμπροσθεν οὔτε προὔβαλεν οὔτ’ ἠπόρησεν οὔτ’ ἐζήτησεν οὔθ’ εὗρεν, ἐγὼ καὶ προβαλῶ καὶ ἀπορήσω καὶ ὅπως χρὴ ζητεῖν ἐρῶ καὶ σὺν θεοῖς εὑρίσκειν ἐλπίζω. τὸ μὲν δὴ πρόβλημα καὶ μικρῷ πρόσθεν εἴρηταί μοι, τί δή ποτε τοσαῦτα δυσπνοιῶν εἴδη παρέλιπεν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης ἐν τῷ προγνωστικῷ τὸ δ’ ἀπόρημα νῦν εἰρήσεται τῶν ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ λόγῳ γεγραμμένων ἀναμνήσαντι πρότερον. ἁπάσας γὰρ τῶν δυσπνοιῶν τὰς διαφορὰς ἐν ἐκείνοις τοῖς λόγοις εἰς πέντε τὰ πάντα γένη διαθέσεών τε καὶ δυσπνοιῶν ἀνήγαγον, ὥστ’ ἀπορώτερον ἔτι τὸ πρόβλημα καθίσταται, τῆς μόνης ἔθ’ ὑπολειπομένης ἐλπίδος ἀνῃρημένης.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: (ἢ) in αὖθις (ἢ) ἀναλεξάμενος

It has indeed been stated both in the writings On the Best Sect and in those of the Therapeutic Method how one ought both to investigate and to discover all such things; but in the first of these commentaries too I showed a twofold path for the discovery of such matters, one setting out from the differences in dyspnoea, the other from the conditions in the respiratory organs, which I said was both easier and clearer; and whoever has read those writings carefully will not need many words for the solution of the present matters. But if he has either engaged with them carelessly, or is otherwise slow to learn, let the careless one, having read them through once more, come back again to these; and let the dull one do something else, knowing 904 that a small nature is not capable of learning thoroughly the thought of a great man, such as Hippocrates was. At any rate, what no one before has either proposed, or raised as a difficulty, or investigated, or discovered, I shall both propose and raise as a difficulty, and I shall say how one ought to investigate it, and with the gods' help I hope to discover it. The problem, then, has already been stated by me a little earlier: why ever did Hippocrates omit so many kinds of dyspnoea in the Prognostic? The difficulty will now be stated, after I have first recalled what was written in the first book. For in those discussions I reduced all the differences of the dyspnoeas to five genera in all, both of conditions and of dyspnoeas, so that the problem becomes still more perplexing, since the only hope still remaining has been removed.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: (ἢ) (in ὁ μὲν ῥᾴθυμος αὖθις (ἢ) ἀναλεξάμενος: bracketed ἢ makes no sense in the construction; omitted in translation), ἀπόρημα (contrasted with πρόβλημα; rendered 'difficulty'), ἀνήγαγον (rendered 'reduced')

It has indeed been stated, both in my writings On the Best Sect and in those of the Therapeutic Method, how one ought both to investigate and to discover all such matters. Moreover, in the first of these present commentaries too I showed a twofold way of discovering such things: one setting out from the differences in difficulty of breathing, the other from the conditions in the respiratory organs, which I said was both easier and clearer. Whoever has read those writings with care will not need many words for the solution of the present questions. But if someone either studied them carelessly, or is otherwise slow to learn, let the careless one read them through again and then come back to these matters; and let the dull one do something else, knowing 904 that a small nature is not able to learn thoroughly the thought of a great man, such as Hippocrates was. At any rate, what no one before me either propounded, or raised as a difficulty, or investigated, or found, I shall propound and raise as a difficulty; I shall say how one ought to investigate it; and with the gods' help I hope to find it. The problem has in fact been stated by me a little earlier: why ever did Hippocrates omit so many kinds of difficulty of breathing in the Prognostic? The difficulty (aporēma) will now be stated, once I have first recalled what was written in the first book. For in those books I reduced all the differences of the difficulties of breathing to five genera in all, both of conditions and of difficulties of breathing, so that the problem becomes still more perplexing, since the only hope still remaining has been taken away.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐν γὰρ τοῖς τοιούτοις ἅπασιν, ἐν οἷς ὅτου δή ποτε τῶν ὄντων διαφοραὶ πλείους μὲν ὑφ’ ἑτέρου τινὸς ἢ ἀνδρὸς ἢ γράμματος, ἥττους δ’ ὑφ’ ἑτέρου λέγονται, μία μόνη τῆς συμφωνίας ἐλπὶς, εἴ τις ἐπιδεῖξαι δύναιτο τὸν μὲν ἐν τοῖς πρώτοις μέρεσι τῆς διαιρέσεως μεμενηκότα, 905 τὸν δὲ καὶ κατατέμνοντα. ταύτης τοίνυν ἡμῶν ἀφῃρημένης τῆς ἐλπίδος (ἐδείχθη γὰρ ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ λόγῳ, μήτε τῶν διαθέσεων μήτε τῶν δυσπνοιῶν εἰς ἀριθμὸν ἐλάττονα δύνασθαι ἀναχθῆναι τὰ γένη) τίς ἂν ἔτι μηχανὴ τῆς λύσεως τῶν νῦν ζητουμένων εὑρεθείη; τά τε γὰρ ἄλλα καὶ πρὸς τῷ παραλελεῖφθαί τινα τῶν πρώτων γενῶν ἔτι καὶ τὸ διεσπάσθαι τε καὶ κακῶς ἀντιδιῃρῆσθαι πρὸς ἄλληλα καὶ ἀσαφῶς ἡρμηνεῦσθαι μέμψαιτ’ ἄν τις ἐν τῷ προγνωστικῷ, καίτοι γ’ εἴπερ τῳ καὶ ἄλλῳ τῶν Ἱπποκράτους βιβλίων ὑπάρχοντι σαφεῖ. καὶ μὴν ἀσαφές τε ἅμα καὶ ἀδιόριστόν ἐστι τὸ κατὰ τὴν ἀρχὴν εὐθὺς εἰρημένον τῆς λέξεως· πνεῦμα δὲ πυκνὸν μὲν ἐὸν πόνον σημαίνει ἢ φλεγμονὴν ἐν τοῖσιν ὑπὲρ τῶν φρενῶν χωρίοισιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀντιδιῃρῆσθαι

For in all such cases, in which the differentiae of any existing thing whatsoever are said by one man or writing to be more numerous, and by another fewer, there is one sole hope of agreement: if someone could show that the one has remained at the first parts of the division, 905 while the other has gone on to subdivide. Now since this hope has been taken away from us (for it was shown in the first book that the genera neither of the conditions nor of the dyspnoeas can be reduced to a smaller number), what device for the solution of the matters now under inquiry could still be found? For, among other things, besides the fact that some of the primary genera have been left out, one might further find fault, in the Prognostic, with their having been torn apart, badly set off against one another in the division, and obscurely expressed—even though that book is clear if any other of Hippocrates' books is. And indeed what is said right at the beginning of the passage is at once unclear and undetermined: "Breath, when it is frequent, signifies pain or inflammation in the regions above the diaphragm."

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κατατέμνοντα, διεσπάσθαι, καίτοι γ’ εἴπερ τῳ καὶ ἄλλῳ τῶν Ἱπποκράτους βιβλίων ὑπάρχοντι σαφεῖ

For in all such cases in which the differences of any existing thing whatsoever are stated as more numerous by one person or one writing, and as fewer by another, there is one sole hope of agreement: if someone could show that the one has remained among the first parts of the division, 905 while the other also cuts it further down. Since, then, this hope has been taken away from us (for it was shown in the first book that neither the genera of the conditions nor those of the dyspnoeas can be reduced to a smaller number), what means could still be found for the solution of the questions now under investigation? For, among other things, besides the omission of some of the primary genera, one might also find fault in the Prognostic with their having been scattered apart, badly contradistinguished from one another, and unclearly expressed, even though it is clear, if ever any other of Hippocrates' books is. And indeed what is said right at the beginning of the text is at once unclear and left undistinguished: 'Breath, when it is frequent, signifies pain or inflammation in the regions above the diaphragm.'

Greek (Kühn)

εἰ μὲν γὰρ μὴ διττὸν ἦν τὸ πυκνὸν πνεῦμα, θάτερον μὲν μετὰ μεγέθους, θάτερον δὲ μετὰ σμικρότητος, ἢ εἰ μὴ διαφόρου διαθέσεως ἑκάτερον αὐτῶν ἔκγονον ὑπῆρχεν, εὐλόγως ἂν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης ἢ ἄτμητον αὐτὸ κατέλιπε, ἢ τοῦ δηλουμένου καθ’ ἑκάτερον τὴν διαφορὰν οὐκ ἐμήνυσεν· ἐπεὶ δ’ αὐτός τε σαφῶς ἐν τοῖς τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν βιβλίοις ἕτερον μὲν 906 τὸ πυκνὸν ἅμα καὶ σμικρὸν, ἕτερον δὲ τὸ πυκνὸν καὶ μέγα γινώσκει, καὶ ἡμεῖς ἀπεδείξαμεν ἐκ τῶν πρώτων εἶναι διαφορῶν αὐτὰ, κακῶς ἄτμητον ἐάσας τὸ πυκνὸν, ἁπλῶς οὕτως εἰπὼν ἠρκέσθη· πνεῦμα δὲ πυκνὸν μὲν ἐὸν πόνον σημαίνει ἢ φλεγμονὴν ἐν τοῖσιν ὑπὲρ τῶν φρενῶν χωρίοισιν. ἓν μὲν δὴ τοῦτ’ ἄπορον. ἐφεξῆς δὲ τῆς τρίτης διαφορᾶς μνημονεύσας ὡδί· μέγα δὲ ἀναπνεόμενον καὶ διὰ πολλοῦ χρόνου, παραφροσύνην σημαίνει· καὶ ταύτην εἰπὼν μόνην σαφῶς τῶν πρώτων διαφορῶν, οὐδ’ ὁτιοῦν τῆς παρακειμένης αὐτῇ τῆς ἀραιόν τε καὶ μικρὸν ἀποδεικνυούσης τὸ πνεῦμα δυσπνοίας ἐμνημόνευσε, καίτοι γε ὥσπερ τὸ πυκνὸν πνεῦμα διττὸν ἦν, μεγέθει καὶ σμικρότητι διαιρούμενον, οὕτω καὶ τὸ ἀραιὸν διττὸν ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἐστὶ, μεγέθει καὶ σμικρότητι διαιρούμενον. θατέρου δὲ μόνου κατὰ τὸ προγνωστικὸν ἐμνημόνευσεν εἰπών· μέγα δὲ ἀναπνεόμενον καὶ διὰ πολλοῦ χρόνου, παραφροσύνην σημαίνει· ἕτερον δὲ τὸ ἀραιὸν ἅμα καὶ σμικρὸν παραλελεῖφθαι φαίνεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For if frequent breath were not twofold, the one kind with largeness, the other with smallness, or if each of them were not the offspring of a different condition, Hippocrates would reasonably either have left it undivided, or would not have indicated the difference of what is signified in each case. But since he himself, in the books of the Epidemics, clearly recognizes the 906 frequent-and-small breath as one thing and the frequent-and-large as another, and we have demonstrated that these belong to the primary differences, he wrongly left the frequent undivided, and was content to say simply thus: "breath, when it is frequent, signifies pain or inflammation in the regions above the diaphragm." This, then, is one difficulty. Next, having mentioned the third difference in this way: "but breath drawn large and at long intervals signifies delirium" – and having stated this one alone clearly among the primary differences, he made no mention whatsoever of the dyspnoea lying next to it, the one that renders the breath rare and small; and yet, just as frequent breath was twofold, divided by largeness and smallness, so too the rare is of necessity twofold, divided by largeness and smallness. But in the Prognostic he mentioned only the one of the two, saying: "but breath drawn large and at long intervals signifies delirium"; the other, the rare-and-small together, appears to have been passed over.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀναπνεόμενον

For if frequent breathing were not twofold, the one kind with largeness, the other with smallness, or if each of them were not the offspring of a different condition, Hippocrates would reasonably either have left it undivided or not have indicated the difference in what is signified by each. But since he himself, in the books of the Epidemics, clearly recognises as one thing 906 the frequent and at the same time small, and as another the frequent and large, and we have demonstrated that these belong to the primary differentiae, he did badly in leaving 'frequent' undivided and contenting himself with saying simply this: 'Breathing, when it is frequent, signifies pain or inflammation in the regions above the diaphragm.' This, then, is one perplexing point. Next, he mentioned the third differentia thus: 'When it is drawn in large and at long intervals, it signifies derangement.' Yet, having stated this one alone of the primary differentiae clearly, he made no mention whatever of the difficulty of breathing adjacent to it, the one that shows the breathing as sparse and small. And yet, just as frequent breathing was twofold, divided by largeness and smallness, so sparse breathing too is of necessity twofold, divided by largeness and smallness. But in the Prognostic he mentioned only one of the two, saying: 'When it is drawn in large and at long intervals, it signifies derangement'; the other, the sparse and at the same time small, appears to have been omitted.

K7.906-907K. VII 906–907, ch. 3.4 difficulty low (0.69/100; thin 1, absent 0) (a) pass (b) fail 2/7 (c) judge_failed_gate unit: mixed

Greek (Kühn)

ὥστ’ ἐκ τῶν τεσσάρων συζυγιῶν, ἃς ἐν τῇ ἐπιδημίᾳ ἔγραψε, τὰς μὲν δύο τὰς πρώτας ἀδιορίστως τε 907 καὶ ἀσαφῶς καὶ συγκεχυμένως εἰρῆσθαι, μόνην δ’, ὡς ἔφαμεν, τὴν τρίτην, αὐτήν τε καὶ τὸ δηλούμενον ὑπ’ αὐτῆς, ἄμφω γεγράφθαι σαφῶς. οὐ μόνον δ’ ἐν τούτοις ἀσαφής τε ἅμα καὶ ἀδιόριστος καὶ παραλιπὼν τὰ πλείω δόξειεν ὑπάρχειν ὁ λόγος, ἀλλὰ κᾀξ ὧν ἐπιφέρων φησί· ψυχρὸν δ’ ἐκπνεόμενον ἔκ τε τῶν ῥινῶν καὶ τοῦ στόματος ὀλέθριον κάρτα ἤδη γίγνεται. τί δήποτε γὰρ, εἰ τὰς κατὰ τὸ θερμόν τε καὶ ψυχρὸν διαφορὰς ἐπελθεῖν ἐγνώκει, τῆς ψυχρᾶς ἐμνημόνευσε μόνης; ἔτι τε τί δή ποτε μὲν τῶν ἄλλων δύο, ἥν τε ἀδιορίστως ὀνομάζει πυκνὸν πνεῦμα καὶ ἣν διοριζόμενος ἀραιὸν καὶ μέγα, τὰς διαθέσεις αὐτὰς μόνας παρέγραψεν, οὐ μὴν εἰς ὅ τι τελευτήσουσιν ἐμήνυσε, πόνον μὲν καὶ φλεγμονὴν ὑπὸ τῆς πυκνῆς, παραφροσύνην δ’ ὑπὸ τῆς ἀραιᾶς τε ἅμα καὶ μεγάλης δυσπνοίας δηλοῦσθαι φάμενος;

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

So that, of the four conjugations which he wrote in the Epidemics, the first two have been stated indeterminately 907 and unclearly and confusedly, and only the third, as we said—both it itself and what is signified by it—has been written clearly. And not only in these matters would the account seem to be unclear and at the same time undetermined and to leave out the greater part, but also from what he says in what follows: "But breath exhaled cold from the nostrils and the mouth is by now very deadly." For why on earth, if he had decided to go through the differences with respect to hot and cold, did he mention the cold one alone? And further, why on earth, in the case of the other two—the one which he names indeterminately "frequent breath" and the one which, making a distinction, he names "rare and large"—did he write down only the conditions themselves, but did not indicate in what they will end, saying that pain and inflammation are signified by the frequent dyspnoea, and delirium by the dyspnoea that is at once rare and large?

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: παρέγραψεν

So that, of the four combinations which he wrote down in the Epidemics, the first two have been stated without distinction, 907 unclearly and confusedly, and only the third, as we said, has been written clearly, both itself and what is indicated by it, the two alike. And not only in these points would his account seem to be unclear and at the same time lacking in distinctions and to have left out the greater part, but also from what he says next: 'but breath exhaled cold from the nostrils and the mouth is already very deadly.' For why ever, if he had resolved to go through the differences relating to hot and cold, did he mention only the cold one? And further, why ever, in the case of the other two, the one which he calls without distinction 'frequent breath' and the one which, drawing a distinction, he calls 'sparse and large', did he note down only the conditions themselves, but did not indicate in what they will end, saying that pain and inflammation are indicated by the frequent difficulty of breathing, and delirium by the one that is sparse and at the same time large?

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς κατὰ τὸ ψυχρὸν διαφορᾶς ἐς ὅ τι μὲν τελευτήσει λέγει· φησὶ γὰρ ὀλέθριον εἶναι κάρτα τὸ τοιοῦτον πνεῦμα· τὴν διάθεσιν δ’ ἀφ’ ἧς οὕτω δυσπνοοῦσιν, ἀσαφεστέραν οὖσαν, οὐκέτι λέγει, βέλτιον ὂν ὀπὶ πασῶν τῶν δυσπνοιῶν καὶ τὰς διαθέσεις αὐτὰς εἰπεῖν, ﻿ 908 αἵ τινές ποθ’ ὑπάρχουσι, καὶ τὸν τρόπον αὐτῶν προσγράψαι, διορίζοντα τὰς ἐπιεικεῖς ἀπὸ τῶν κακοηθῶν. οὐ τοίνυν οὕτως ἐποίησεν, ἀλλ’ ἐπὶ μὲν τῶν προτέρων δυσπνοιῶν τὰς διαθέσεις, ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς ἐσχάτης τὸν τρόπον μόνον ἐδήλωσεν. ὃ δὴ πάντων ἀφυκτότατόν ἐστι τῶν ἐγκλημάτων, τὸ μηδὲν ὅλως εἰρῆσθαι περὶ τῆς ἀνωμάλου δυσπνοίας, ἤδη δὴ καὶ αὐτὸ προσκείσθω τῷ λόγῳ. τὸ μὲν γὰρ ἐκτεῖνον καὶ κατεπεῖγον πνεῦμα καὶ τὸ ἔξω μέγα καὶ εἴσω μικρὸν, οὐκ εἴρηται μὲν οὐδ’ αὐτὰ διὰ τοῦ προγνωστικοῦ γράμματος, ἐξ ὧν δὲ ἡμεῖς ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ λόγῳ διήλθομεν, ἐν ταῖς τέσσαρσι τῶν δυσπνοιῶν συζυγίαις εὑρίσκεται περιεχόμενα· τὴν δέ γε πέμπτην ἀνώμαλον, ἧς τὸ μὲν ἥμισυ μέρος ἐν τοῖς τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν ἔγραψε, διπλῆν εἴσω φάμενος ἐπανάκλησιν, ἐν δὲ τῷ προγνωστικῷ τελέως παρέλιπεν, οὐκ ἔστιν ὑπ’ οὐδεμιᾶς τῶν τεττάρων συζυγιῶν κατ’ οὐδένα τρόπον εὑρεῖν περιεχομένην. αἵ μὲν οὖν ἀπορίαι τοσαῦταί τε καὶ τοιαῦται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὀπὶ, ἐκτεῖνον

But in the case of the difference according to cold he does say in what it will end; for he says that such breath is exceedingly destructive; the condition, however, from which they breathe with difficulty in this way, being the more obscure, he no longer states, although it would have been better, in the case of all the dyspnoeas, both to state the conditions themselves, 908 whatever they are, and to add to them their manner, distinguishing the mild from the malignant. He did not, then, do this, but in the case of the former dyspnoeas he indicated the conditions, in the case of the last only the manner. And what is the most inescapable of all the charges, that nothing whatever has been said about uneven dyspnoea, let this too now be added to the account. For the stretching-out and pressing breath, and that which is large outward and small inward, are indeed not themselves stated in the text of the Prognostic, but from what we went through in the first book they are found to be contained in the four conjugations of dyspnoeas; the fifth, however, the uneven, half of which he wrote in the books of the Epidemics, speaking of a double inward recall, but which in the Prognostic he omitted completely, it is not possible to find contained under any of the four conjugations in any manner whatever. The difficulties, then, are so many and of such a kind.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀπὶ (apparently a misprint for ἐπὶ), ἐκτεῖνον, κατεπεῖγον, τὸν τρόπον (mode / character of the course), συζυγίαις

In the case of the difference according to cold, however, he does state what it will end in: for he says that such breath is very deadly. But the condition from which people have difficulty in breathing in this way, which is more obscure, he no longer states, although it would have been better, for all the difficulties of breathing, both to state the conditions themselves, 908 whatever they may be, and to add in writing their mode, distinguishing the mild ones from the malignant. He did not, however, do so; rather, for the earlier difficulties of breathing he indicated the conditions, and for the last only the mode. And what is the most inescapable of all the charges, that nothing at all has been said about irregular difficulty of breathing, let this too now be added to the account. For the extended and urgent breath, and the breath that is large outward and small inward, are themselves not mentioned in the Prognostic writing either; yet, from what we went through in the first book, they are found to be contained in the four pairings of difficulties of breathing. But the fifth, the irregular one, half of which he wrote about in the books of the Epidemics, speaking of a double inward recall, but which he omitted entirely in the Prognostic, cannot by any means be found contained under any of the four pairings. Such, then, and so many are the difficulties.

K7.908-909K. VII 908–909, ch. 3.5 difficulty mid (1.9/100; thin 2, absent 1) (a) fail (b) fail 5/8 (c) judge_failed_gate unit: fail

Greek (Kühn)

Χρὴ δὲ τοὐντεῦθεν μὴ τοῖς πολλοῖς τῶν ἀντιλεγόντων Ἱπποκράτους παραπλησίως, καὶ ἡμᾶς τὰς μὲν 909 ἀπορίας εὐπόρως ἐξευρίσκειν, ἐν δὲ ταῖς λύσεσιν αὐτῶν ἀπορεῖν, ἀλλὰ πρῶτον μὲν ὁδόν τινα τῆς εὐπορίας εὑρεῖν, ἐφεξῆς δ’ ἀποδείξει πιστώσασθαι τὰ δι’ αὐτὴν εὑρεθέντα. τίς οὖν ὁδὸς τῆς εὐπορίας; χρὴ γὰρ ἐντεῦθεν ἄρξασθαι· μία μὲν ἡ κοινὴ πάντων τῶν παλαιῶν συνήθης βραχυλογία· δευτέρα δὲ Ἱπποκράτους αὐτοῦ μεγαλοφροσύνη. δέδεικται γὰρ ἡμῖν δεινὸς πολλάκις ὁ ἀνὴρ οὗτος, ὅσα μὲν καὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις ἰατροῖς ἐστι γνώριμα παρατρέχων, ὡς ὑπὸ μηδενὸς τῶν γνησίως ἀσκούντων τὴν τέχνην ἀγνοηθῆναι δυνάμενα, τὰ δὲ τοῖς τοιούτοις ἀγνοούμενα πάντ’ ἐπιμελῶς τε ἅμα καὶ σαφῶς ἐξηγούμενος. εἰ δὴ τοῦτ’ ἐστὶν ἔθος αὐτῷ, τί θαυμαστὸν ἀμφισβητουμένην τε καὶ ἀσαφῆ τοῖς πολλοῖς δυσπνοίας διαφορὰν τὴν κατὰ μέγεθός τε καὶ ἀραιότητα διά τε τῶν ἄλλων συγγραμμάτων ἐπιδεῖξαι πειραθῆναι παραφροσύνης ἰδίαν οὖσαν ἔν τε τῷ προγνωστικῷ σαφοῦς ἀξιῶσαι διδασκαλίας, τὰς δὲ τῆς πυκνότητος, ὡς ἂν μηδὲ ταύτας ἀγνοηθῆναι τοῖς ἰατροῖς δυναμένας, ἑνὶ κεφαλαίῳ περιλαβόντα, διὰ ταχέων δηλῶσαι;

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

From this point on, however, we must not, like the majority of those who contradict Hippocrates, ourselves also readily discover the 909 difficulties while being at a loss in their solutions; rather we must first find some path toward resolution, and next confirm by demonstration the things found by means of it. What, then, is the path of resolution? For one must begin from here. One is the brevity of expression common to all the ancients and customary among them; a second is the high-mindedness of Hippocrates himself. For this man has often been shown by us to be formidable in passing over whatever is known also to the other physicians, as things that cannot be unknown to any of those who genuinely practise the art, while expounding carefully and at the same time clearly all the things that are unknown to such men. If, then, this is his custom, what wonder is it that the difference of dyspnoea in respect of greatness and rarity, which is disputed and unclear to most people, he attempted through his other writings to show to be peculiar to delirium (paraphrosyne), and in the Prognostic deemed worthy of clear teaching, whereas the differences of frequency, since these too cannot be unknown to physicians, he embraced under a single heading and indicated quickly?

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

From this point on, however, we must not, like the majority of those who contradict Hippocrates, find the 909 difficulties readily and then be at a loss when it comes to solving them; rather, we must first find some road to their resolution, and then confirm by demonstration what has been found by means of it. What, then, is the road to resolution? For this is where we must begin. One is the brevity of expression that was customary and common to all the ancients; the second is the loftiness of mind of Hippocrates himself. For we have often shown that this man is skilled at passing over whatever is familiar to other physicians as well, as things that could not be unknown to anyone who genuinely practises the art, while explaining carefully and clearly all the things that are unknown to such people. If, then, this is his custom, what wonder is it that the difference of dyspnoea in respect of largeness and infrequency, which is disputed and unclear to most, he both tried to show through his other writings to be peculiar to derangement and in the Prognostic deemed worthy of clear teaching, while the differences in respect of frequency, since these too could not be unknown to physicians, he comprised under a single head and set out quickly?

Greek (Kühn)

ἔν τε γὰρ πλευρίτισι καὶ περιπνευμονίαις καὶ συνάγχαις καὶ 910 ὀρθοπνοίαις πυκνὸν καὶ μικρὸν γίνεται τὸ πνεῦμα· καὶ εἰ μὴ πλευρῖτις δ’ εἴη, μόνη δ’ ὀδύνη κατὰ τὰς πλευρὰς ὑπάρχοι, πυκνὸν καὶ μικρὸν ἔσται τὸ πνεῦμα, καθάπερ εἰ καὶ περὶ τὸ στόμα τῆς κοιλίας, ἢ τὸν στόμαχον, ἢ τοῦ σπληνὸς τὴν κεφαλὴν, ἢ τοῦ ἥπατος τὰ κυρτά. καὶ γὰρ οὖν κᾀνταῦθα πόνου τινὸς ὑπάρχοντος, ἀνάγκη μικρὸν καὶ πυκνὸν γενέσθαι τὸ πνεῦμα, καὶ πολύ γε μᾶλλον, εἰ περὶ τὸ διάφραγμα γένοιτο καὶ τὸ συνάπτον αὐτοῦ τῷ περιτοναίῳ καὶ τοὺς μῦς τοὺς περικειμένους αὐτόθι. καίτοι κᾂν εἰ μὴ τῇδε μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ κατωτέρω μειζόνως πεπονθὸς τὸ περιτόναιον, αἰσθάνεταί τε τῆς βλάβης αὐτοῦ τὸ διάφραγμα καὶ οὕτω μικρὸν καὶ πυκνὸν ἐργάζεται τὸ πνεῦμα. καὶ τῆς γαστρὸς αὐτῆς αἱ μείζους διαθέσεις, αἱ σὺν ὀδύνῃ τε καὶ ὄγκῳ, καὶ τοῦ σπληνὸς τὰ κάτω καὶ τοῦ ἥπατος, οὐδὲν ἧττον καὶ αὐταὶ πυκνὸν καὶ μικρὸν ἐργάζονται τὸ πνεῦμα· καὶ ὅλως, εἴτ’ ὄγκος τις εἴη, θλίβων τε καὶ στενοχωρῶν ὁτιοῦν τῶν ἀναπνευστικῶν ὀργάνων, εἴτ’ ὀδύνη περί τι μόριον οὕτω πλησιάζον τοῖς ἀναπνευστικοῖς, ὡς κινουμένοις αὐτοῖς συγκινεῖσθαι, πυκνὸν καὶ μικρὸν ἔσται τὸ πνεῦμα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For in pleurisies and peripneumonias and anginas and 910 orthopnoeas the breath becomes frequent and small; and even if there should be no pleurisy, but only pain in the region of the ribs, the breath will be frequent and small, just as also if [there is pain] about the mouth of the stomach, or the oesophagus, or the head of the spleen, or the convex parts of the liver. For indeed here too, when some pain is present, the breath must necessarily become small and frequent, and much more so if it should arise about the diaphragm and the part of it that joins the peritoneum and the muscles that lie around there. And indeed, even if the peritoneum has been affected more severely not only here but also lower down, the diaphragm perceives its injury and in this way makes the breath small and frequent. And the greater affections of the belly itself, those accompanied by pain and swelling, and [those of] the lower parts of the spleen and of the liver, no less make the breath, they too, frequent and small; and in general, whether there be some swelling pressing upon and constricting any whatsoever of the respiratory organs, or pain about some part so near to the respiratory organs that it is moved along with them when they move, the breath will be frequent and small.

Greek (Kühn)

τὰς δ’ ἄλλας αἰτίας 911 τούτων ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ γράμματι διήλθομεν, ὥσπέρ γε καὶ ὅτι θερμασίας πολλῆς ἐν τοῖς ἀναπνευστικοῖς ὀργάνοις ἠθροισμένης, πυκνὸν καὶ μέγα γενήσεται τὸ πνεῦμα. φαίνεται δὲ καὶ τοῦτο σχεδὸν ἁπάσης ἡμέρας ἐπὶ τῶν διακαῶν πάντων πυρετῶν, ὥστ’ οὐδ’ αὐτὸ διαλάθοι τινά. καὶ μέν τοι καὶ ὡς τὴν παρὰ φύσιν φλόγωσιν οὐχ Ἱπποκράτης μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ οἱ ἄλλοι σχεδὸν ἅπαντες οἱ πρὸ Ἐρασιστράτου φλεγμονὴν ὠνόμαζον, οὐχ ἡμῖν μόνοις, ἀλλὰ καὶ σύμπασιν ὡς εἰπεῖν τοῖς Ἱπποκράτους ἐξηγηταῖς ἀποδέδεικται. τούτων οὖν ὑποκειμένων, οὐδὲν ἔτι θαυμαστὸν ἑνὶ κεφαλαίῳ τὰς εἰρημένας ἀμφοτέρας διαφορὰς δηλῶσαι τὸν Ἱπποκράτην, πνεῦμα δὲ πυκνὸν μὲν ἐὸν, εἰπόντα, πόνον σημαίνει ἢ φλεγμονὴν ἐν τοῖσιν ὑπὲρ τῶν φρενῶν χωρίοισιν, ὡς ἂν καὶ ἡμῶν νοῆσαι δυναμένων, ὅτι τὸ μὲν μετὰ μικρότητος πόνον σημαίνει, τὸ δὲ μετὰ μεγέθους φλεγμονὴν, ὅ πέρ ἐστι φλόγωσιν, δηλοῖ· προσθέντα δ’ εὐλόγως, ἐν τοῖσιν ὑπὲρ τῶν φρενῶν χωρίοισιν, ἤτοι τοῖς ἀναπνευστικοῖς, ὡς ἂν τῶν κάτω τοῦ διαφράγματος ἀμέτρων θερμασιῶν εἰς μέγεθός τε καὶ πυκνότητα τὴν ἀναπνοὴν ἐξᾶραι μὴ δυναμένων.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

The other causes 911 of these we went through in the first book, just as we also showed that, when much heat has been gathered in the respiratory organs, the breath will become frequent and large. And this too is seen almost every day in all burning fevers, so that it too could not escape anyone's notice. Moreover, that not Hippocrates alone but also nearly all the others before Erasistratus called the unnatural burning (phlogosis) 'phlegmone' has been demonstrated not by us alone but by practically all the exegetes of Hippocrates. These things, then, being laid down, it is no longer at all surprising that Hippocrates indicated both the aforesaid differentiae under a single heading, saying 'breath, when it is frequent, signifies pain or phlegmone in the regions above the phrenes', since we too are able to understand that the breath that is frequent together with smallness signifies pain, whereas that which is frequent together with largeness indicates phlegmone, that is, phlogosis; and that he reasonably added 'in the regions above the phrenes', that is, the respiratory regions, since immoderate heats below the diaphragm are unable to raise the respiration to largeness and frequency.

The other causes 911 of these we went through in the first book, just as we also showed that, when much heat has accumulated in the respiratory organs, the breath will become frequent and large. This too is evident almost every day in all burning fevers, so that it could not escape anyone's notice either. Moreover, not only we ourselves but practically all the commentators on Hippocrates have demonstrated that not only Hippocrates but also almost all the others before Erasistratus called burning heat (phlogōsis) contrary to nature 'phlegmonē'. These things being laid down, then, it is no longer surprising that Hippocrates indicated both of the aforementioned differences under a single heading, when he said: 'breath, being frequent, signifies pain or phlegmonē in the regions above the phrenes'. He said this on the assumption that we too are able to understand that the frequent breath with smallness signifies pain, whereas that with largeness indicates phlegmonē, which is to say phlogōsis. He reasonably added 'in the regions above the phrenes', that is, in the respiratory parts, since immoderate heats below the diaphragm are unable to raise the breathing to largeness and frequency.

912 This, then, is the way of discovery of the things under inquiry, at least as regards the primary differences; but it is necessary to add some demonstration as well to the argument. For at present it has been shown that it is not impossible that Hippocrates held this opinion; but it will be shown next that this is altogether the case. For why ever is it that in both books of the Epidemics, in the second and in the sixth, he is found dividing off these two differences of dyspnoea first of all, the former being small and frequent breath, the second frequent and large, whereas in the passage before us he is thought to be ignorant of them—in addition to the fact that immediately after these words, in the Prognostic, he writes 'rare and large'? Does he then know rare and large breath, but is ignorant of frequent and small?

Greek (Kühn)

κᾂν φαίη τις τῶν τὰ χείρω σπουδαζόντων, ὡς αὐτὸ μὲν τί ποτ’ ἐστὶ πυκνὸν καὶ μικρὸν πνεῦμα γινώσκειν ἠδύνατο, τίς δ’ ἐστὶν ἡ ἀπεργαζομένη διάθεσις αὐτὸ, μὴ γινώσκειν ἠδύνατο, ἀλλ’ ὅταν εἴποι, τὸ πυκνὸν πνεῦμα πόνον σημαίνει ἢ φλεγμονὴν ἐν τοῖς ὑπὲρ τῶν φρενῶν χωρίοις, δῆλός ἐστι καὶ τὰς διαθέσεις γράφων ﻿ 913 καὶ οὐδετέραν παραλιπὼν, ἀλλὰ κατὰ τὴν αὐτὴν τάξιν ἐδήλωσεν, ἣν κᾀν τοῖς δύο τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν βιβλίοις ἐποιήσατο. δῆλον οὖν ἐστι τῷ μὲν πυκνῷ καὶ σμικρῷ τὸν πόνον, τῷ δὲ πυκνῷ καὶ μεγάλῳ τὴν φλόγωσιν συνάπτων. οὐδὲ γὰρ τοῦτ’ ἔστιν εἰπεῖν, ὡς ἐν μὲν τοῖς τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν δύο ἐμνημόνευσε μικροῦ καὶ πυκνοῦ καὶ μεγάλου καὶ πυκνοῦ πνεύματος, ἑτέρωθι δ’ οὐδαμόθι. καὶ γὰρ ἐν αὐτοῖς ἐκείνοις πολλάκις κᾀν τῷ περὶ διαίτης ὀξέων, ὃν ἐπιγράφουσι περὶ πτισάνης, εὐθὺς ἐν ἀρχῇ τοῦ συγγράμματος οὐ μόνον διαῤῥήδην ὀνομάζει τὸ τῆς δυσπνοίας ταύτης εἶδος, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὴν διάθεσιν ἐφ’ ᾗ γίγνεται καὶ τὸν τρόπον αὐτῆς καθ’ ὃν βλάπτειν πέφυκε, καίτοι τῶν ὑπὸ φιλονεικίας πολλὰ παρακοπτόντων ὡς οὐ γνήσια τοῦ Ἱπποκράτους βιβλία οὐδεὶς ἐτόλμησεν ἀφελέσθαι τὸν ἄνδρα τουτὶ τὸ σύγγραμμα. τὰ μὲν γὰρ ἐπὶ τέλει τὰ μετὰ τὴν τοῦ λουτροῦ χρῆσιν ἀφαιροῦσι πολλοὶ, τὰ δὲ ἕως τοῦδε φυλάττουσιν ἅπαντες. ἂν οὖν μὴ μόνον ἐν τούτῳ τῷ βιβλίῳ δείξωμεν, ἀλλὰ καὶ δι’ ἄλλων πολλῶν ὁμοίως εὑρεθῇ τάς τε διαφορὰς τοῦ πυκνοῦ πνεύματος γινώσκων καὶ τὰς διαθέσεις, ἐνδεῖν τι δόξει πρὸς τὴν ἀπόδειξιν;

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And even if one of those who are zealous for the worse should say that he was able to know what in itself frequent and small breathing is, but was not able to know what the condition is that produces it, nevertheless, when he says 'frequent breathing signifies pain or inflammation in the regions above the diaphragm', he is clearly writing down the conditions as well 913 and omitting neither of them, but has indicated them in the same order which he also adopted in the two books of the Epidemics. It is clear, therefore, that he is joining pain to the frequent and small breathing, and burning heat (phlogosis) to the frequent and large. For neither is it possible to say this, that in the two books of the Epidemics he made mention of small and frequent and of large and frequent breathing, but nowhere else. For both in those very books, often, and in On Regimen in Acute Diseases, which they entitle On Ptisan, right at the beginning of the treatise he not only expressly names this kind of dyspnoea, but also the condition in which it arises and the manner in which it is of a nature to do harm; and yet, among those who out of contentiousness strike out many books as not genuine works of Hippocrates, no one has dared to take this treatise away from the man. For the parts at the end, those after the use of the bath, many remove, but the parts up to that point all preserve. If, then, we show not only in this book, but he is also found likewise throughout many others, to know both the differences of frequent breathing and the conditions, will anything seem to be lacking for the demonstration?

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μήτε

But if, in the 914 Prognostic itself as well, he should be found further on neither ignorant of the differences and also acquainted with the conditions, would you not believe still more that the matter proposed has been demonstrated? And indeed, that these things are so and that we are saying nothing false, one may examine from the passages themselves. I shall begin from On Regimen in Acute Diseases. In this book, right at the beginning, when making his discourse about the diseases of the lung and the thorax, he says somewhat as follows: "For in those in whom food has just been shut in, if one gives the gruel without first evacuating downward, one would further exacerbate the pain that is present, and if it is not present one would at once produce it, and the breathing would become more frequent; and this is bad, for it is drying to the lung and wearying to the hypochondria and the lower belly and the diaphragm." In this passage he has shown, first, that frequent breathing also comes about on account of pain, leaving it to us, I think, to conceive the manner of its genesis, which I think I have clearly gone through in the first of these commentaries.

Greek (Kühn)

καθ’ ὃν γὰρ τρόπον ὀδυνώμενοι τὰ σκέλη μέγα προβαίνειν οὐ δυνάμεθα, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον, ὅταν πονῇ τι τῶν κινουμένων ἐν ταῖς ἀναπνοαῖς ὀργάνων, μικρὸν ἀναπνεῖν ἀναγκαζόμεθα 915 καὶ θᾶττον παύσασθαι γλιχόμεθα. ἐπεὶ γὰρ συμπαρεκτείνεται τῷ μήκει τοῦ χρόνου τῆς ἀναπνοῆς ὁ πόνος, ὁ θᾶττον ἂν ἐπισχὼν τὴν κίνησιν τῶν ἀναπνευστικῶν θᾶττον ὀδυνώμενος παύσεται. ἀλλ’ ὅσον πρωϊαίτερον τοῦ δέοντος ἐπαύσατο, τοσοῦτον τὸ ζῶον ἐλλιπέστερον ἀπήλαυσε τῆς εἰσπνοῆς. ὥσπερ οὖν εἴ τις ἐλάττω τῆς χρείας σιτία προσάρηται, ταχέως οὗτος ἑτέρων δεήσεται σιτίων, οὕτως εἴ τις εἰσπνεύσειεν ἔλαττον τῆς χρείας, θᾶττον οὗτος εἰσπνοῆς προσδεήσεται, κᾀν τῷδε συμβήσεται πυκνοῦσθαι τὸ πνεῦμα. ὃν γὰρ ἡσύχαζε χρόνον ἐν τῷ μεταξὺ τῶν ἐνεργειῶν ἀναπαυόμενα τὰ τῆς ἀναπνοῆς ὄργανα, τοῦτον νῦν αὐτοῖς οὐκ ἐπιτρέπει ἡσυχάζειν τὸ ζῶον. ἅτε γὰρ ἐλλιπῶς ἀναπνέον ἑκάστην ἀναπνοὴν, ἀεὶ καὶ μᾶλλον ἐγγυτέρω πελάζει πνίξεως· δέει δὲ αὐτοῦ τοῦδε τοῦ παθήματος ἀναπνεῦσαι θᾶττον ἐπείγεται, καὶ κινεῖ τὰ τῆς ἀναπνοῆς ὄργανα πρωϊαίτερον τοῦ δέοντος, οὐκ ἐπιτρέπον αὐτοῖς ἡσυχάζειν ἐς ὅσον ἡσύχαζεν, ὅτ’ εἶχε κατὰ φύσιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For in the same way that, when we have pain in our legs, we cannot take a large step, in the same way, when any of the organs that are moved in respirations is in pain, we are compelled to breathe small 915 and we are eager to stop sooner. For since the pain is coextensive with the length of the time of the respiration, he who would sooner check the motion of the respiratory organs will sooner cease being in pain. But by as much as he has stopped earlier than he ought, by so much has the animal enjoyed the inspiration more deficiently. Just as, then, if someone takes in less food than his need, he will quickly require further food, so if someone should inspire less than his need, he will sooner require a further inspiration, and in this it will come about that the breathing becomes frequent. For the time during which the organs of respiration were at rest, reposing in the interval between the activities, this time the animal now does not permit them to rest. For inasmuch as it breathes each respiration deficiently, it draws ever and ever nearer to suffocation; and from fear of this very affection it hastens to breathe sooner, and moves the organs of respiration earlier than it ought, not permitting them to rest for as long as it rested when it was in accordance with nature.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

For just as, when we are in pain in the legs, we cannot take long strides, in the same way, whenever one of the organs that are moved in breathing is in pain, we are compelled to breathe small 915 and we long to stop more quickly. For since the pain is prolonged along with the length of time of the breath, whoever checks the motion of the respiratory organs more quickly will more quickly cease to be in pain. But by as much as he stopped earlier than he should, by so much has the animal had a more deficient enjoyment of inspiration. Just as, then, if someone takes less food than his need requires, he will quickly need more food, so if someone breathes in less than his need requires, he will sooner need a further inspiration, and in this way it will come about that the breathing becomes frequent. For the time during which the organs of breathing used to rest, reposing in the interval between their activities, the animal now does not allow them to rest. For since it breathes deficiently with each breath, it draws ever nearer and nearer to suffocation; and from fear of this very affection it hastens to breathe more quickly, and moves the organs of breathing earlier than it should, not allowing them to rest for as long as they used to rest when it was in its natural state.

Greek (Kühn)

ὅταν οὖν διὰ τὴν ὀδύνην ὁ Ἱπποκράτης πυκνότερον τὸ πνεῦμα γενέσθαι λέγῃ, φαίνηται δὲ διὰ μέσης τῆς μικρότητος ἡ πυκνότης γεννᾶσθαι, 916 παντί που δῆλον, ὡς καὶ τὴν σμικρότητα γινώσκει. τίς γὰρ ἂν τὸ τρίτον ἀπὸ τοῦ πρώτου δύναιτο γνῶναι τὸ μέσον ἀγνοῶν, καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν αὐτὸ τῷ τρίτῳ τῆς γενέσεως αἴτιον ὑπάρχῃ; καὶ μέν γε καὶ προ ελθὼν ὀλίγον αὐτοῖς τοῖς ἰδίοις ῥήμασιν ἄντικρυς αἰτιᾶται τὴν μικρότητα τῆς πυκνότητος, γράφων ὡδί· καὶ γὰρ αὐτὸ τὸ πτύελον ἐνεχόμενον κωλύει μὲν τὸ πνεῦμα εἴσω φέρεσθαι, ἀναγκάζει δὲ ταχέως ἔξω φέρεσθαι. τὴν ἑτέραν ἐνταῦθα πάλιν αἰτίαν διδάσκει τῆς τοῦ πυκνοῦ πνεύματος γενέσεως, ὠνόμαζε δ’ αὐτὴν ἐν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν λόγοις στενοχωρίαν τῶν ἀναπνευστικῶν ὀργάνων. ὅπερ οὖν ἐπὶ τοῦ πόνου παρέλιπεν ἡμῖν ἐπινοῆσαι, τοῦτ’ ἐνταῦθα αὐτὸς ἐξηγήσατο. τί δ’ ἐστὶ τοῦτο; πολλοῦ, φησι, τοῦ πτυέλου περιεχομένου κατὰ τὰς ἀρτηρίας, τοῦ πνεύμονος δηλονότι, καὶ τὴν χώραν τοῦ πνεύματος αὐτοῦ φθάνοντος κατειληφέναι, κωλύεται κατὰ τὴν τῆς εἰσπνοῆς ἐνέργειαν εἴσω παραγενέσθαι τὸ πνεῦμα. ἆρ’ οὖν ἕτερόν τι τῆς μικρᾶς εἰσπνοῆς ἐστι τὸ νῦν λεγόμενον;

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὠνόμαζε

When, therefore, Hippocrates says that the breath becomes more frequent on account of the pain, and the frequency is seen to be generated by way of smallness as the intermediate term, 916 it is surely clear to everyone that he also recognises the smallness. For who could know the third thing from the first while being ignorant of the middle, and especially when that middle is itself the cause of the third's coming to be? And indeed, going on a little further, in his own very words he outright assigns the smallness as the cause of the frequency, writing as follows: for the sputum itself, being held fast, prevents the breath from being carried inward, and compels it to be carried outward quickly. Here again he teaches the other cause of the generation of frequent breath, and he named it in the preceding discussions narrowness of the respiratory organs. What, then, in the case of pain he left for us to work out, this he himself here expounded. And what is this? When much sputum, he says, is contained in the arteries—those of the lung, evidently—and has already taken possession of the space belonging to the breath itself, the breath is prevented from arriving within during the activity of inspiration. Is, then, what is now being said anything other than a small inspiration?

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προ ελθὼν (printed divided in Kühn; read as προελθὼν), ὠνόμαζε (subject unclear: Hippocrates, or Galen's own earlier books; rendered 'he called'), ἀρτηρίας (the air-ducts of the lung; kept as 'arteries'), φθάνοντος κατειληφέναι

When, therefore, Hippocrates says that the breath becomes more frequent on account of the pain, and it is evident that the frequency is generated by way of the smallness as intermediate, 916 it is surely clear to everyone that he also recognises the smallness. For who could know the third from the first while ignorant of the middle, and above all when this middle is the cause of the third's coming into being? And indeed, going on a little further, in his very own words he expressly makes the smallness the cause of the frequency, writing thus: 'For the sputum itself, being held fast, prevents the breath from being carried inwards, and compels it to be carried outwards quickly.' Here again he teaches the other cause of the generation of frequent breathing; he called it in the earlier discussions narrowness of the respiratory organs. What, then, he left to us to work out for ourselves in the case of the pain, this he has here explained himself. And what is this? When much sputum, he says, is contained in the arteries (those of the lung, plainly), and has already occupied the space of the breath itself, the breath is prevented from coming inwards during the activity of inspiration. Is what is now being said, then, anything other than small inspiration?
