Greek (Kühn)

οὐ μὴν, εἴγε τῷ πλήθει τῆς εἰσπνεομένης οὐσίας παραμετρεῖν χρὴ τὸ μέγεθος τῆς ἀναπνοῆς, ὡς ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ 917 δέδεικται λόγῳ· ἀλλὰ εἰ δι’ ἄλλην τινὰ αἰτίαν ἀναγκάζεσθαι ταχέως ἔξω φέρεσθαι τὸ πνεῦμά φησι ἢ διὰ τὴν μικρότητα τῆς εἰσπνοῆς, οὐκ ἔστιν οὐδὲ τοῦτ’ εἰπεῖν, ἐχούσης οὕτω τῆς λέξεως· καὶ γὰρ αὐτὸ τὸ πτύελον ἐνεχόμενον, κωλύει μὲν τὸ πνεῦμα εἴσω φέρεσθαι, ἀναγκάζει δὲ ταχέως φέρεσθαι ἔξω. διότι γὰρ ἐστερήθη τῆς εἴσω φορᾶς, ἡσυχάζειν οὐκ ἐπιτρέπει τὸ ζῶον, πνιγῆναι κινδυνεῦον, κᾀν τῷδε πυκνὸν γίνεται. τὸ γὰρ ἐπὶ τὴν ἐκπνοὴν ἰέναι ταχέως ἀπὸ τῆς εἰσπνοῆς οὐδὲν ἕτερόν ἐστι τοῦ πυκνῶς ἀναπνεῖν. οὐ γὰρ δὴ τάς γε συλλαβὰς τῶν ὀνομάτων αὐτὰς ἐθέλειν ἀκούειν προσήκει, τῶν λόγων ὅλων σαφῶς λεγομένων, οὐδ’ ἄλλο μέν τι νομίζειν εἶναι τὸ ταχέως ἐπὶ τὴν ἐκπνοὴν ὁρμᾷν τοῦ πυκνῶς ἀναπνεῖν, ἄλλο δέ τι τὸ κωλύεσθαι τὴν εἰσπνοὴν αὐτάρκως γίγνεσθαι τοῦ μικρὰν εἶναι τὴν ἀναπνοήν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Certainly not, if indeed one must measure the magnitude of respiration by the quantity of the substance inspired, as has been shown in the first 917 book. But if someone says that he (Hippocrates) states that the breath is compelled to be carried outward quickly for some other cause than the smallness of the inspiration, it is not possible to say this either, since the wording runs thus: for the sputum itself, being retained, prevents the breath from being carried inward, and compels it to be carried outward quickly. For because it has been deprived of the inward movement, it does not permit the animal, being in danger of suffocating, to remain at rest, and in this way it (the breath) becomes frequent. For to proceed quickly from the inspiration to the expiration is nothing other than to breathe frequently. For indeed one ought not to insist on hearing the very syllables of the words, when the statements as a whole are expressed clearly, nor to think that rushing quickly to expiration is one thing and breathing frequently another, nor that the inspiration's being prevented from taking place sufficiently is one thing and the respiration's being small another.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἡσυχάζειν οὐκ ἐπιτρέπει τὸ ζῶον, πνιγῆναι κινδυνεῦον

Not at all, if indeed one must measure the magnitude of breathing by the quantity of the substance inspired, as has been shown in the first 917 book. But if he says that the breath is compelled to be carried outward quickly through some other cause than the smallness of inspiration, not even this can be said, since the text runs thus: 'For the sputum itself, being held fast, prevents the breath from being carried inward, and compels it to be carried outward quickly.' For because it has been deprived of its inward motion, the animal, being in danger of being choked, does not allow it to rest, and in this way it becomes frequent. For to go quickly from inspiration to expiration is nothing other than to breathe frequently. For one ought not to wish to attend to the very syllables of the words when the statements as a whole are clearly expressed, nor to think that rushing quickly to expiration is one thing and breathing frequently another, or that inspiration's being prevented from taking place sufficiently is one thing and breathing's being small another.

Greek (Kühn)

ἀλλ’ ἐνταῦθα μὲν αὐτὸς ὁ Ἱπποκράτης ἐξηγήσατο σαφῶς, ὅπως ἐπὶ τῷ πτυέλῳ κατεχομένῳ πυκνὸν γίνεται τὸ πνεῦμα διὰ μέσης τῆς μικρότητος· ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς ὀδύνης παρέλιπεν, ὡς ἂν, οἶμαι, πολλαχοῦ μὲν αὐτὸς εἰρηκὼς κωλύεσθαι τὰς ἐνεργείας ﻿ 918 τῶν ὀργάνων ὑπὸ τοῦ πόνου, δυναμένων δὲ καὶ ἡμῶν, κᾂν εἰ μὴ παρ’ ἐκείνου προσηκούσαμεν, ἐκ τῶν ἐναργῶς φαινομένων εὑρεῖν αὐτό. πρὸς τῷ τοίνυν ἄμφω ταῦθ’ ἡμᾶς ἐν τῷδε τῷ λόγῳ διδάξαι σαφῶς, ὡς καὶ πόνος καὶ στενοχωρία μικρὰν ἐργαζόμενα τὴν ἀναπνοὴν, εὐθὺς καὶ πυκνὴν δείκνυσιν, ἔτι καὶ τοῦτ’ ἐκδιδάσκει, τὸ μὴ ταῦτ’ εἶναι μόνον πυκνοῦ πνεύματος αἴτια. πρῶτον μὲν γὰρ ἐν τῷ φάναι, εἴρηται δ’ οὕτως ἐπὶ τῆς ὀδύνης, αὐτὸ δῆλον ποιεῖται πυκνὸν ἤδη διὰ τὴν ἑτέραν αἰτίαν ὑπάρχον τὸ πνεῦμα, πυκνότερον γίγνεσθαι λέγων διὰ τὸν πόνον, ὡς ἂν, οἶμαι, κᾀνταῦθ’ ἡμῶν λογίσασθαι δυναμένων, τὴν πυρεκτικὴν θερμασίαν πάντως αὐτὸ πυκνὸν ἀπεργάσασθαι· δεύτερον δ’ ἐν τῷ λέγειν, ὅτι ὑπὸ τοῦ πυκνοῦ καὶ πολλοῦ πνεύματος, ὡς ἤδη εἴρηται, γλισχραινόμενον τὸ πτύελον ἀπέπτως κωλύει τὴν ἐπάνοδον γίνεσθαι. τοῦ μὲν γὰρ πυκνοῦ πνεύματος ὁ πόνος αἴτιον, τοῦ πολλοῦ δὲ οὐχ οὗτος, ἀλλ’ ἡ πυρεκτικὴ θερμασία. πάνυ δὲ κᾀνταῦθα σαφῶς, οὐ μεγάλου πνεύματος εἶπεν, ἀλλὰ πολλοῦ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπάνοδον, εἴρηται δ’ οὕτως ἐπὶ τῆς ὀδύνης, δείκνυσιν

But here Hippocrates himself explained clearly how, in the case of sputum that is held back, the breathing becomes frequent by way of smallness as the intermediate step; but in the case of pain he left it out, inasmuch, I think, as he had himself said in many places that the activities 918 of the organs are hindered by pain, and as we too are able, even if we had not heard it from him, to discover this from what is evidently apparent. Accordingly, besides teaching us clearly both these things in this passage—that both pain and narrowness of space, by making the respiration small, immediately show it to be frequent as well—he further teaches this too, that these are not the only causes of frequent breathing. For first, in what he says—and it is put thus in the case of pain—he makes this evident, saying that the breathing, being already frequent on account of the other cause, becomes more frequent on account of the pain, inasmuch, I think, as here too we are able to reason out that the febrile heat in any case renders it frequent; and second, in saying that the sputum, made viscous in an unconcocted state by the frequent and abundant breath, as has already been said, prevents the return from taking place. For pain is the cause of the frequent breath, but of the abundant breath the cause is not pain but the febrile heat. And here too, quite clearly, he did not say 'great' breath but 'abundant'.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: εἴρηται δ’ οὕτως ἐπὶ τῆς ὀδύνης, διὰ μέσης τῆς μικρότητος, ἀπέπτως, ἐπάνοδον, προσηκούσαμεν

But here Hippocrates himself explained clearly how, when the sputum is held back, the breathing becomes frequent by way of the intermediate smallness; in the case of the pain, however, he left it out, since, I think, he himself has said in many places that the functions 918 of the organs are hindered by pain, and since we too are able, even if we had not heard it from him beforehand, to discover it from what plainly appears. Besides, then, teaching us clearly both these things in this passage, namely that pain and narrowness of space alike, by making the breathing small, at once show it to be frequent as well, he further teaches this too: that these are not the only causes of frequent breathing. First, in saying (and it is said thus in the case of the pain), he makes it plain that the breathing is already frequent because of the other cause, when he says that it becomes more frequent because of the pain, since, I think, here too we are able to reason out that the febrile heat in any case makes it frequent. Second, in saying that the sputum, being made viscid by the frequent and copious breathing, as has already been said, prevents its upward passage from taking place, being unconcocted. For of the frequent breathing the pain is the cause, but of the copious breathing it is not this but the febrile heat. And here too he quite clearly said not 'large breathing' but 'copious'.

Greek (Kühn)

τὸ μὲν γὰρ μέγα τῷ καθ’ ἑκάστην ἀναπνοὴν ποσῷ κρίνεται, τὸ δὲ πολὺ δυνατόν ἐστιν ἐκ μικρῶν ἐνίοτε πολλῶν 919 ἀθροίζεσθαι. καὶ φαίνεταί γε σαφῶς τοῦτο γιγνόμενον ἐπὶ τῶν πλευριτικῶν, μέγα μὲν εἰσάπαξ ἀναπνεῖν μὴ δυναμένων διὰ τὸν πόνον, καίτοι γε δέονται πλέονος ἀέρος διὰ τὸν πυρετὸν, τῇ συνεχείᾳ δὲ τῆς ἐνεργείας ἴσον ταῖς μεγάλαις ἀναπνοαῖς τὸ πλῆθος τοῦ πνεύματος ἀθροιζόντων. ὃ γὰρ ὀλίγαι μεγάλαι, τοῦτο πολλαὶ σμικραὶ δύνανται. κατὰ δὲ ταὐτὰ κᾀπὶ τῶν περιπνευμονικῶν πολλοῦ μὲν τοῦ πνεύματος ἡ χρεία, διὰ δὲ τὴν στενοχωρίαν ὀλίγον εἰσπνεῖται· κᾀνταῦθ’ οὖν ἡ πυκνότης πολλὰς ἐργαζομένη τὰς μικρὰς ἀναπνοὰς, εἰς ἴσον ἄγει ταῖς ὀλίγαις τε καὶ μεγάλαις. Ἆρ οὖν ἔτι δεησόμεθα τὰς συλλαβὰς εὑρίσκειν τῶν ὀνομάτων ἐν τῷδε τῷ βιβλίῳ γεγραμμένας ἐφεξῆς οὕτω, μικᾶς τε καὶ μεγάλης ἀναπνοῆς, οὐκ ἀρκούμενοι τοῖς πράγμασιν αὐτοῖς οὕτω σαφῶς δηδηλωμένοις;

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μικᾶς (read μικρᾶς), δηδηλωμένοις (read δεδηλωμένοις)

For 'great' is judged by the quantity in each respiration, whereas 'much' can sometimes be accumulated from many small ones. 919 And this is clearly seen to happen in pleuritics, who are unable to respire greatly at a single stroke because of the pain—although indeed they need more air because of the fever—but who by the continuity of the activity accumulate an amount of breath equal to that of great respirations. For what few great ones can do, many small ones can do. In the same way, in peripneumonics too the need is for much breath, but because of the narrowness little is inhaled; here too, then, the frequency, by making the small respirations many, brings them to equality with the few and great ones. Shall we, then, still need to find the syllables of the terms written in this book in just such a sequence—'small' and 'great respiration'—not being content with the things themselves so clearly demonstrated?

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μικᾶς (read as μικρᾶς), δηδηλωμένοις (read as δεδηλωμένοις), εἰσάπαξ

For 'large' is judged by the quantity in each breath, whereas 'much' can sometimes be accumulated from many small ones 919. And this is clearly seen to happen in pleuritic patients: they cannot breathe large at a single stroke because of the pain, although they need more air because of the fever, but by the continuity of the activity they accumulate a quantity of breath equal to that of large breaths. For what few large breaths can do, many small ones can do. In the same way, in peripneumonic patients too there is need of much breath, but because of the narrowness little is inhaled; here too, then, the frequency, by making the small breaths many, brings them to equality with the few and large. Shall we then still need to find the syllables of the words written in this book in sequence, thus: 'of small and of large breathing', not being content with the things themselves, so clearly shown?

καὶ μὴν οὐ τοῦτ’ ἦν τὸ ζητούμενον, εἰ τοὔνομα γινώσκει τὸ τῆς μικρᾶς καὶ μεγάλης ἀναπνοῆς Ἱπποκράτης· εὑρίσκεται γὰρ ἔν τε τοῖς τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν βιβλίοις γεγραμμένον κᾀν τῷ προγνωστικῷ πάλιν ἐν οἷς ἔλεγε· μέγα δὲ ἀναπνεόμενον καὶ διὰ πολλοῦ 920 χρόνου παραφροσύνην σημαίνει· ἀλλ’ εἰ διττὸν οἶδε τὸ πυκνὸν πνεῦμα καὶ τὸ μὲν αὐτῶν ὑπὸ πόνου γίγνεσθαί φησι, τὸ δὲ ὑπὸ θερμασίας πολλῆς ἐν τοῖς ἀνπνευστικοῖς ὀργάνοις ἀθροιζομένης. ὅπου τοίνυν ἐπιδέδεικται ταῦτα, περιττὸν ἔτι ποθεῖν ἀκούειν τὰς τῶν ὀνομάτων συλλαβὰς, ἐπεὶ καὶ κατ’ αὐτὸν τοῦτον τὸν ἐν τῷ προγνωστικῷ λόγον, ὃν ἐπὶ τῆς παραφροσύνης ἔγραψεν, ἐξέσται τινὶ λέγειν οὐκ ἀραιὸν μὲν καὶ μέγα τὸ δηλοῦν αὐτὴν εἰρῆσθαι πνεῦμα, καὶ τὴν λέξιν προτείνεσθαι τόνδε τὸν τρόπον ἔχουσαν, μέγα δὲ ἀναπνεόμενον καὶ διὰ πολλοῦ χρόνου παραφροσύνην σημαίνει, ὥσπερ οὐ ταὐτὸν μόνον ἢ ἀραιὸν εἰπεῖν, ἢ διὰ πολλοῦ χρόνου, ἢ νῦν ἡμῶν ὀνόματα θηρευόντων, καὶ οὐ τὰ πράγματα ζητούντων. ἀλλ’ ἐπειδή τινες εἰς τοσοῦτον ἥκουσι σκαιότητος, ὡς καὶ τῶν ὀνομάτων ἀκούειν ἐθέλειν αὐτῶν, ἀναμνήσωμεν καὶ αὐτοὺς ἐξ αὐτοῦ τοῦ περὶ διαίτης ὀξέων βιβλίου δυοῖν τινων ῥήσεων ὡδί πως ἐχουσῶν· πνεῦμα προσπταῖον ἐν τῇ ἄνω φορῇ πυκνὸν ἢ μέγα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προσπταῖον

And indeed this was not the question at issue, whether Hippocrates knows the name of small and great respiration; for it is found written both in the books of the Epidemics and again in the Prognostic, in the passage where he said: "breathing that is great and at long intervals of 920 time signifies delirium"; but rather whether he knows the dense breath to be twofold, and says that one of them arises from pain, the other from much heat gathered in the respiratory organs. Since, then, these things have been demonstrated, it is superfluous still to long to hear the syllables of the names, since even with regard to this very statement in the Prognostic, which he wrote concerning delirium, it will be open to someone to say that the breath indicating it was not described as rare and great, and to put forward the wording as having the following form, "breathing that is great and at long intervals of time signifies delirium" — as if it were not merely the same thing to say either "rare" or "at long intervals of time", or as if we were now hunting names and not inquiring into the things themselves. But since some have arrived at such a degree of obtuseness as to wish to hear the very names themselves, let us remind them too, from the book On Regimen in Acute Diseases itself, of two passages which run roughly as follows: "breath stumbling in its upward movement, dense or great."

And yet this was not the question at issue, whether Hippocrates knows the name of small and large breathing; for it is found written both in the books of the Epidemics and again in the Prognostic, in the words where he said: 'breath drawn large and at long 920 intervals signifies derangement'. The question was rather whether he knows that frequent breath is twofold, and says that one kind of it arises from pain, the other from much heat collecting in the respiratory organs. Since, then, these things have been demonstrated, it is superfluous still to long to hear the syllables of the names. For even in respect of this very statement in the Prognostic, which he wrote about derangement, it will be open to someone to say that it is not sparse and large breath that has been stated to indicate it, and to put forward the passage as running in the following way: 'breath drawn large and at long intervals signifies derangement', as though it were not one and the same thing to say either 'sparse' or 'at long intervals', or as though we were now hunting for names and not seeking the things themselves. But since some have come to such a pitch of perversity as to want to hear even the names themselves, let us remind them too, from the very book On Regimen in Acute Diseases, of two passages that run roughly as follows: 'breath stumbling in its upward course, frequent or large.'

ἐνταῦθα μὲν ἔχεις, εἴπερ ἐπιθυμεῖς, τοὔνομα τὸ μέγα· τὸ δ’ αὖ μικρὸν, ἐπειδάν φησι· καὶ πνεῦμα 921 σμικρὸν καὶ ἀντίσπασις τοῦ πνεύματος πυκνὴ καὶ βιαίη παραγίγνεται. κατὰ δ’ αὖ τὸ προγνωστικὸν αὐτὸ δυσπετέως φέρειν τὴν νοῦσόν φησι καὶ πνεῦμα μέγα καὶ πυκνὸν εἶναι. τοῦτο μὲν οὖν ἄντικρυς αὐτοῖς τοῖς ὀνόμασιν ἔγραψε, μέγα καὶ πυκνὸν, τὸ δ’ αὖ μικρὸν καὶ πυκνὸν ἐν τῷ περὶ συνάγχης λόγῳ, πλεῖστόν τε πόνον παρέχουσι καὶ ὀρθόπνοιαν, εἰπών. ὅτι γὰρ ἐν ταῖς ὀρθοπνοίαις μικρὸν καὶ πυκνὸν γίνεται τὸ πνεῦμα διὰ τὴν στενοχωρίαν τῶν ἀναπνευστικῶν ὀργάνων, οὐκ οἶδ’ εἴ τις ἀγνοεῖ. καίτοι κᾂν εἰ τὰ τοιαῦτα παρ’ Ἱπποκράτους αὐτοῦ μαθεῖν ἐθέλομεν, ἐν μὲν τῷ περὶ διαίτης ὀξέων ἐν αἷς ὀλίγον ἔμπροσθεν ἔγραψα λέξεσιν ἐδίδαξεν ἡμᾶς, ὅπως γίγνεται μικρὸν καὶ πυκνὸν πνεῦμα κατὰ τὴν τῶν πτυέλων ἐπίσχεσιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀντίσπασις

Here, then, you have, if you desire it, the word "great"; and the word "small" in turn, when he says: "and small breath 921 and a frequent and violent drawing-back of the breath supervenes." And again in the Prognostic itself he says that the patient bears the disease with difficulty and that the breath is great and frequent. This, then, he wrote outright in the very words, "great and frequent"; but the "small and frequent" in turn in the account of angina, when he said, "they produce very great distress and orthopnoea." For that in orthopnoeas the breath becomes small and frequent because of the narrowness of the respiratory organs, I do not know whether anyone is ignorant. And yet, even if we wish to learn such things from Hippocrates himself, in the Regimen in Acute Diseases, in the words I wrote out a little earlier, he taught us how small and frequent breath comes about in connection with the retention of the sputa.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀντίσπασις, δυσπετέως

Here, if you desire it, you have the word 'large'; and 'small', in turn, where he says: 'and the breath 921 small, and a frequent and violent counter-drawing of the breath supervenes.' Again, in the Prognostic itself he speaks of bearing the disease with difficulty and of the breath being large and frequent. This, then, he wrote outright in the very words, 'large and frequent'; and 'small and frequent', in turn, in his account of synanche, saying: 'they cause very great pain and orthopnoea.' For I do not know whether anyone is unaware that in orthopnoeas the breath becomes small and frequent because of the narrowness of the respiratory organs. And yet, if we wish to learn such things from Hippocrates himself, in On Regimen in Acute Diseases, in the passages I wrote out a little earlier, he taught us how small and frequent breath arises with the retention of the sputa.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐν δὲ τῷ περὶ ἄρθρων εὐθὺς ἐν ἀρχῇ τοῦ περὶ τῆς ῥάχεως αὐτοῦ, τὰς ὑπὸ τῶν ὄγκων ἢ νοσημάτων γιγνομένας κυφώσεις ἐν τοῖς ἀνωτέρω τῶν φρενῶν χωρίοις διηγούμενος, ὡδί πως φησὶν, ( ὅλην γὰρ, οἶμαι, κάλλιον εἶναι δοκεῖ παραγράψαι τὴν ῥῆσιν) οἷσι μὲν ἄνω τῶν φρενῶν τὸ κυφὸν, τουτέοισι μὲν αἱ πλευραὶ οὐκ ἐθέλουσιν εἰς τὸ εὐρὺ αὔξεσθαι, 922 ἀλλ’ ἐς τοὔμπροσθεν, τό τε στῆθος ὀξὺ γίγνεται, ἀλλ’ οὐ πλατὺ, αὐτοί τε δύσπνοοι γίγνονται καὶ κερχνώδεες· ἧσσον γὰρ ἰθυωρίην ἔχουσιν αἱ κοιλίαι τὸ πνεῦμα δεχόμεναι καὶ προπέμπουσαι. καὶ γὰρ δὴ καὶ ἀναγκάζονται κατὰ τὸν μέγαν σπόνδυλον λορδὸν τὸν αὐχένα ἔχειν, ὡς μὴ προπετὴς ᾖ αὐτέοισιν ἡ κεφαλή. στενοχωρίην οὖν πολλὴν τῇ φάρυγγι παρέχουσι· καὶ τοῦτο συμβάλλεται εἰς τὸ εἴσω ῥέπον. καὶ γὰρ αὐτοῖσιν ὀρθοῖσι φύσει δύσπνοιαν παρέχει τοῦτο τὸ ὀστέον, ἂν ἔσω ῥέπει, ἔστ’ ἂν ἀναπιεσθῇ. διὰ οὖν τὸ τοιοῦτον πρῆγμα ἐξεχέμβροχοι οἱ τοιοῦτοι τῶν ἀνθρώπων μᾶλλον φαίνονται ἢ ὑγιέες, φυματίαι τε ὡς ἐπὶ πολὺ τὸν πνεύμονά εἰσιν οἱ τοιοῦτοι, σκληρῶν φυμάτων καὶ ἀπέπτων. καὶ γὰρ ἡ πρόφασις τοῦ κυφώματος καὶ ἡ ξύντασις τοῖσι πλείστοισι διὰ τοιαύτας συστροφὰς γίγνεται, οἷσιν ἂν κοινωνήσωσιν οἱ ξύντονοι. ἐν τούτῳ τῷ λόγῳ παντὶ τὴν μὲν κύφωσιν ἐπὶ τοῖς ἐν πνεύμονι φύμασιν ἀπέπτοις τε καὶ σκληροῖς γίγνεσθαι λέγει, τὴν δὲ δύσπνοιαν ἐπὶ τῇ στενοχωρίᾳ· κερχνώδη δὲ κέκληκε τὴν τοιαύτην διαφορὰν αὐτοῦ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐξεχέμβροχοι, ξύντονοι, συστροφὰς, τοῦτο συμβάλλεται εἰς τὸ εἴσω ῥέπον

But in On Joints, right at the beginning of his account of the spine, when he is describing the kyphoses that come about from swellings or diseases in the regions above the diaphragm, he says something like this (for I think it better to write out the whole passage): 'In those in whom the hump is above the diaphragm, the ribs are not inclined to grow in breadth, 922 but forwards, and the chest becomes pointed, but not broad, and they themselves become dyspnoeic and wheezing (kerchnōdeis); for the cavities that receive and send forth the breath have less straightness of direction. For indeed they are also compelled to hold the neck lordotic at the great vertebra, so that their head may not fall forward. They therefore produce much narrowing for the pharynx; and this contributes to the inward inclination. For even in those who are naturally straight this bone produces dyspnoea, if it inclines inward, until it is pressed back. On account of such a condition, then, such people appear more prominent-throated (exechembrochoi) than healthy people, and such people are for the most part subject to phymata in the lung, hard and unconcocted phymata. For indeed the cause of the kyphosis and the tension arise in most cases through such gatherings (systrophai), with which the tense cords (xyntonoi) may be in communication.' In this whole passage he says that the kyphosis comes about upon unconcocted and hard phymata in the lung, and the dyspnoea upon the narrowing; and he has called such a variety of it 'kerchnōdēs' (wheezing).

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐξεχέμβροχοι, οἱ ξύντονοι, συστροφὰς, ξύντασις, ἰθυωρίην, συμβάλλεται εἰς τὸ εἴσω ῥέπον

And in On Joints, right at the beginning of his section on the spine, where he describes the kyphoses that arise from swellings or diseases in the regions above the diaphragm, he speaks somewhat as follows (for I think it seems better to transcribe the whole passage): "In those in whom the hump is above the diaphragm, the ribs will not grow into breadth, 922 but forwards, and the chest becomes pointed, but not broad, and they themselves become dyspnoeic and wheezy; for the cavities that receive the breath and send it onwards have less of a straight course. And indeed they are also compelled to hold the neck bent backwards (lordotic) at the great vertebra, so that the head may not be pitched forward for them. This, then, produces much narrowness for the throat; and this also contributes to its inclining inwards. For even in those who are naturally upright this bone, if it inclines inwards, produces dyspnoea until it is pressed back up. On account of such a condition, then, people of this kind appear to have protruding windpipes rather than to be healthy, and such people are for the most part tuberculous in the lung, with hard and unconcocted tumours. For indeed the cause of the hump and of the contraction in most cases comes about through such concretions, in which the tensed [cords] come to share." In this whole passage he says that the kyphosis arises from the unconcocted and hard tumours in the lung, and the dyspnoea from the narrowness; and he has called this sort of variety of it 'wheezy'.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐν δέ γε τῷ ﻿ 923 περὶ διαίτης ὀξέων, ἡνίκα τῶν εἰς μικρότητά τε καὶ πυκνότητα τὴν ἀναπνοὴν τρεπομένων ἐμέμνητο, καὶ αὐτὸ μὲν τοῦτο τὸ τῆς ὀρθοπνοίας ὄνομα προσέθηκε, καὶ ῥέγχειν αὐτοὺς ἔφησεν, ὥστε κᾀπειδὰν ἐν προγνωστικῷ περὶ συνάγχης διηγούμενος εἶπε· πλεῖστόν τε πόνον παρέχουσι καὶ ὀρθόπνοιαν, οὐκ ἄλλο τι δυσπνοίας εἶδος ἢ τὸ σμικροῦ καὶ πυκνοῦ πνεύματος οὕτως ὀνομάζει. πάντες γὰρ οἱ διὰ στενοχωρίαν αὐτάρκως εἰσπνεῖν μὴ δυνάμενοι πνίγεσθαι κινδυνεύουσιν, ἢν κατακαίωνται. καὶ διὰ τοῦτ’ ὀρθὸν ἔχειν δέονται τόν τε θώρακα σύμπαντα καὶ τὸν τράχηλον, ὡς ἂν οὐ σμικρᾶς αὐτοῖς βοηθείας ἐκ τοῦ τοιούτου σχήματος προσερχομένης. εὐρύτερά τε γὰρ ἅμα καὶ μακρότερα σύμπαντα τὰ τῆς ἀναπνοῆς ὄργανα γίνονται, τοῦτο μὲν ἐξευθυνόμενα πρὸς τῶν ὀρθίων σχημάτων, τοῦτο δ’ ἧττον εἰς ἑαυτὰ καταπίπτειν κωλυόμενα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κατακαίωνται, ἧττον εἰς ἑαυτὰ καταπίπτειν κωλυόμενα

But in the 923 Regimen in Acute Diseases, when he made mention of those whose respiration turns to smallness and frequency, he both added this very name of orthopnoea and said that they snore (ῥέγχειν), so that also when, in the Prognostic, giving his account of angina, he said 'they cause very great pain and orthopnoea', he is naming in this way no other kind of difficulty of breathing than that of small and frequent breath. For all who because of narrowness are unable to breathe in sufficiently are in danger of being suffocated if they lie down. And for this reason they need to hold the whole thorax and the neck upright, since no small help comes to them from such a posture. For all the organs of respiration become at once both wider and longer, on the one hand being straightened out by the upright postures, and on the other being prevented from collapsing as much into themselves.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ῥέγχειν, ἧττον εἰς ἑαυτὰ καταπίπτειν κωλυόμενα

And in the 923 On Regimen in Acute Diseases, when he made mention of those whose breathing turns to smallness and frequency, he both added this very name of orthopnoea and said that they rattle in the throat; so that also when, in the Prognostic, treating of synanche, he said 'they cause very great distress and orthopnoea', he calls by this name no other kind of difficulty of breathing than that of small and frequent breath. For all who, through narrowness of space, are unable to breathe in sufficiently are in danger of choking if they lie down. And for this reason they need to keep both the whole thorax and the neck upright, since no small help comes to them from such a posture. For all the organs of respiration together become at once wider and longer, on the one hand being straightened out by the upright postures, on the other being hindered so that they collapse less into themselves.

K7.923-924K. VII 923–924, ch. 3.7 difficulty high (4.26/100; thin 7, absent 1) (a) pass (b) fail 5/8 (c) judge_failed_gate unit: mixed

Greek (Kühn)

δῆλος δὲ καὶ αὐτὸς ὁ Ἱπποκράτης οὕτω γινώσκων ὑπὲρ αὐτῶν, ὡς ἡμεῖς νῦν λέγομεν, ἐξ ὧν φησιν ἐν τοῖς περὶ ἄρθρων· αὐτοί τε δύσπνοοι γίνονται καὶ κερχνώδεες, ἧσσον γὰρ ἰθυωρίην ἔχουσιν αἱ κοιλίαι αἱ τὸ πνεῦμα δεχόμεναι καὶ προ 924 πέμπουσαι· πᾶσιν οὖν τοῖς διὰ στενοχωρίαν δυσπνοοῦσιν καὶ μικρὸν γίνεται τὸ πνεῦμα καὶ δύο ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἕπεται συμπτώματα, τό τε κερχνῶδες εἶδος τῆς ἀναπνοῆς, ὃ δὴ καὶ ῥέγχειν ὀνομάζεται, καὶ τὸ μικρῷ πρόσθεν εἰρημένον, ἡ ὀρθόπνοια. καὶ δὴ ὥσπερ ἐξεπίτηδες διατέμνων αὐτὰ τὸ μὲν ἕτερον ἐν προγνωστικῷ γράφει ταῖς ὀλεθρίαις συνάγχαις προσεῖναι λέγων, τὸ δὲ δεύτερον ἐν τῷ περὶ ἄρθρων, ἐν μὲν τῷ προγνωστικῷ τὴν ὀρθόπνοιαν, ἐν δὲ τῷ περὶ ἄρθρων τὸ κερχνῶδες· ἄμφω δὲ ἐν τῷ περὶ διαίτης ὀξέων ἐς ταὐτὸν ἤγαγεν, οὕτως γράψας ὁ Ἱπποκράτης· οἱ δ’ ὑπὸ τῆς ὀρθοπνοίας καὶ τοῦ ῥέγχους ἀποπνιγέντες. ὃ γὰρ ἐν τῷ περὶ ἄρθρων κέρχνον, τοῦτ’ ἐνταῦθα ῥέγχος ὠνόμασε. χρῆται δὲ καὶ ἀλλαχόθι τῶν συγγραμμάτων ἀμφοτέροις τοῖς ὀνόμασιν, ὡς ταὐτὸν δυναμένοις δηλοῦν. ἐπειδὴ οὖν οὕτως ἐπιμελῶς αὐτὸς ἐν τῷ περὶ διαίτης περὶ πάσης γενέσεως πυκνοῦ πνεύματος διεξῆλθε, θαυμαστὸν οὐδὲν, εἰ μόνον τὸ κεφάλαιον αὐτὸ τοῦ παντὸς λόγου γέγραφεν ἐν τῷ προγνωστικῷ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κερχνώδεες, κερχνῶδες, ῥέγχους, κέρχνον

And Hippocrates himself is plainly of the same mind about these matters as we are now saying, from what he says in the books On Joints: 'and they themselves become dyspnoeic and rasping (kerchnōdees), for the cavities that receive and send 924 forth the breath have less straightness.' In all, then, who are dyspnoeic because of narrowness, the breath both becomes small and two symptoms follow of necessity: the rasping (kerchnōdes) kind of respiration, which is also named stertor (rhenchein), and the one mentioned a little earlier, orthopnoea. And indeed, as though deliberately dividing them, he writes of the one in the Prognostic, saying that it is present in the deadly anginas, and of the second in On Joints—in the Prognostic orthopnoea, in On Joints the rasping; but both he brought together into one in On Regimen in Acute Diseases, Hippocrates writing thus: 'those who have been suffocated by orthopnoea and stertor (rhenchos).' For what in On Joints he called kerchnos, this he here named rhenchos. And elsewhere too in his writings he uses both names as able to signify the same thing. Since, therefore, he himself went through so carefully in On Regimen every origin of frequent breath, it is no wonder if in the Prognostic he has written only the summary itself of the whole account.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἰθυωρίην, κερχνώδεες, κέρχνον, ῥέγχους, ῥέγχειν

Hippocrates himself also clearly held this view of them, just as we are now saying, as appears from what he says in On Joints: "they themselves become dyspnoeic and wheezing, for the cavities that receive the breath and send 924 it onward have less straightness." In all, then, who breathe with difficulty because of narrowness, the breath becomes small, and two symptoms follow of necessity: the wheezing kind of respiration, which is also called rattling, and the one mentioned a little earlier, orthopnoea. And indeed, as if dividing them on purpose, he writes the one in the Prognostic, saying that it attends deadly synanchai, and the second in On Joints: in the Prognostic orthopnoea, in On Joints the wheezing. But in On Regimen in Acute Diseases Hippocrates brought both together, writing thus: "those who were choked by orthopnoea and rattle." For what in On Joints he called wheeze (kerchnos), here he named rattle (rhenchos). Elsewhere in his writings too he uses both names as able to signify the same thing. Since, then, he himself in On Regimen went through so carefully every origin of frequent breath, it is no wonder if in the Prognostic he wrote only the sum total itself of the whole account.

Greek (Kühn)

ὅτι δ’ ἐπιμελῶς 925 διῆλθε πάντα περὶ πυκνοῦ πνεύματος λόγον, οὐδὲν ἔτι δέομαι πλεῖον λέγειν, ἀλλ’ ἀναμνήσας τοῦτο μόνον, ὡς ἤτοι διὰ πόνον, ἢ στενοχωρίαν, ἢ πλῆθος θερμασίας, πυκνὸν γίνεται τὸ πνεῦμα, τὴν ὅλην ῥῆσιν ἐφεξῆς παραγράψω τόνδε τὸν τρόπον ἔχουσαν· οἷσί τε γὰρ σιτία αὐτίκα ἐγκατακέκλεισται, ἢν μή τις ὑποκενώσας τὸ ῥόφημα δοίη, τὴν ὀδύνην ἐνεοῦσαν προσπαροξύνειεν ἂν, καὶ μὴ ἐνεοῦσαν εὐθὺς ἐμποιήσειεν ἂν, καὶ πυκνότερον γένοιτ’ ἂν τὸ πνεῦμα· κακὸν δὲ τοῦτο· ξηραντικὸν γὰρ πνεύμονος, καὶ κοπῶδες ὑποχονδρίου καὶ ἤτρου καὶ φρενῶν. ἢν δέ τι τῆς ὀδύνης τοῦ πλευροῦ ξυνεχέος ἐούσης καὶ πρὸς τὰ θερμάσματα μὴ χαλώσης καὶ τοῦ πτυέλου μὴ ἀπιόντος, ἀλλὰ καὶ καταγλισχραινομένου ἀσαπέως, ἢν μὴ λύσῃ τις τὴν ὀδύνην, ἢ κοιλίην μαλθάξας, ἢ φλέβα τεμὼν, ὁπότερον ἂν τουτέων σημαίνῃ, τὰς δὲ πτισάνας ἢν οὕτως ἔχουσι διδῷ, ταχέες οἱ θάνατοι τῶν τοιουτέων γίνονται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀσαπέως, τι (in ἢν δέ τι)

But that he carefully 925 went through the whole account concerning frequent breath, I need say nothing further; rather, having recalled only this, that the breath becomes frequent either through pain, or through narrowness of space, or through an abundance of heat, I shall next set down the whole passage, which runs in the following manner: "For in those in whom food has just been shut in, if one gives the gruel without first evacuating somewhat, one would further exacerbate the pain if it is present, and if it is not present one would straightaway produce it, and the breath would become more frequent; and this is bad, for it is drying to the lung and wearying to the hypochondrium and the lower belly and the diaphragm. And if, the pain of the side being continuous and not relaxing under the fomentations, and the sputum not coming away but rather becoming viscous without concoction (ἀσαπέως), one does not release the pain, either by softening the belly or by cutting a vein, whichever of the two of these is indicated, and yet gives the ptisans to those in such a condition, the deaths of such patients come quickly."

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τι (in ἢν δέ τι τῆς ὀδύνης: syntax anacoluthic), ἀσαπέως, ἐνεοῦσαν, ὑποκενώσας, ἤτρου

That he went 925 carefully through the whole account of rapid breathing, I need say nothing further; instead, having recalled only this, that breathing becomes rapid either on account of pain, or narrowness of space, or an abundance of heat, I shall next set out the whole passage, which runs as follows: “For those in whom food has just been shut up within, if one gives the gruel without first evacuating from below, it would further exacerbate the pain if it is present, and if it is not present would at once produce it, and the breathing would become more rapid; and this is bad, for it is drying to the lung, and fatiguing to the hypochondrium, the lower belly and the diaphragm. And if, while the pain of the side is continuous and does not relax under the warm applications, and the sputum does not come away but even becomes viscid without being concocted, one does not resolve the pain, either by softening the bowel or by cutting a vein, whichever of these is indicated, but gives the ptisans to those in this state, the deaths of such patients come swiftly.”

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: βλητοὺς, πνευματίαι

On account of these causes, then, and others of this kind, those who use whole ptisan die even more readily on the seventh day or in fewer days, some having also been somewhat impaired in their mind 926, others suffocated by orthopnoea and rattling. The ancients very much called such persons 'struck' (blētoi), chiefly for this reason, and not least because, when they have died, the side is found livid, somewhat like a blow. The cause of this is that they die before the pain has been resolved. For they quickly become short of breath (pneumatiai), and by the frequent and copious breath, as has already been said, the sputum, becoming viscous in an uncocted state, prevents its return from taking place, but rather produces the rattling, being held fast in the bronchi of the lung. When it comes to this, it is for the most part deadly. For the sputum itself, being retained, prevents the breath from being carried inward, and compels it to be carried out quickly, and thus they assist one another toward the harm. For the retained sputum makes the breath frequent, and the breath, being frequent, makes the sputum more viscous and prevents it from slipping away. If anyone should attend carefully to this passage and read it over many times, I expect that he will readily be persuaded of what has been said.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: βλητοὺς, πνευματίαι, ξυντιμωρέουσι, γνώμην, ὀλιγημερώτεροι

For these reasons, then, and others of the same kind, those who use whole ptisans die even more often on the seventh day or within fewer days, some having also suffered some damage to their judgement 926, others choked by orthopnoea and by rattling. The ancients were especially wont to call such persons 'struck' (blētoi), chiefly for this reason, and not least because, when they have died, the side is found livid, something like a blow. The cause of this is that they die before the pain has been resolved. For they quickly become short of breath (pneumatiai), and, as has already been said, by the frequent and copious breathing the sputum, becoming viscid without concoction, prevents its upward passage from taking place, but, being held fast in the bronchi of the lung, produces the rattle. When it comes to this, it is for the most part deadly. For the sputum itself, being held fast, prevents the breath from being carried inward and compels it to be carried outward quickly, and so they assist one another toward the bad outcome. For the sputum, being held fast, makes the breathing frequent, and the breathing, being frequent, makes the sputum more viscid and prevents it from slipping away. If anyone attends carefully to this passage and reads it over many times, I hope that he will easily be persuaded by what has been said.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For because the division of dense breathing into the differences within it did not 927 require great wisdom, and because he had already written it out completely in this book, for this reason in the Prognostic he ran over it very quickly, making as it were a kind of epitome of the account, as for people who already know the whole matter. Three conjugations of dyspnoeas, then, have now been discovered by us, and the fourth remains, in which the breath is small and rare. Why ever, then, did he omit it and write of cold expiration? Or is it because the breath that is at once small and rare is not one thing and the cold another? And this is clear. For we showed that its opposite, the breath that is at once large and dense, is the offspring of immoderate heat gathered in the respiratory organs. Of necessity, then, the small and rare breath will belong to these same organs when they have been cooled, and two difficulties are solved for us, in that neither the fourth conjugation of dyspnoea, the small and rare, has been omitted, nor the hot.

For we shall have that one included in the other part of the dense breath spoken of at the beginning, since it is the same as the dense and 928 large; and the rare and small, in turn, in the cold, for this one too does not differ from the cold. Why, then, did he write it not as rare and small but as cold? Because, first, he wished to indicate it from the more readily apparent symptom; second, to point out the cause on account of which he calls it deadly; and third, because he supposed that in this way we would most quickly understand both that this very one differs in no way from the rare and small, and that its opposite, the hot, differs in no way from the dense and large. For the cold breath is in every case both rare and small, as has also been shown in the second book; and as for the dense and large, that which is indicative of burning heat (phlogosis) of the respiratory organs, no one would even rightly speak of it as such unless it were also breathed out hot. And indeed, by adding, to the cold breath alone, that it 'now becomes exceedingly deadly', he clearly shows the opinion he holds concerning the three dyspnoeas spoken of before. For if he considered any of those too to be deadly, he would surely have added this somewhere. Did he then suppose them to be free of danger? By no means; rather, not deadly, yet not for that reason free of danger either.

For the respiratory 929 organs' being chilled is outright deadly, since the heart is situated there, which we know to be the origin and fount of the innate heat; but that there should be either much burning heat (phlogosis) in them, or pain, or delirium, is not necessarily deadly; for heat of this kind accompanies many of the acute fevers, and it has been stated before in how many and in which diseases pain is present. And indeed delirium, too, is among the things not necessarily deadly. That this is his judgement about them he showed clearly through the statement that follows, saying: "And one must consider that easy breathing has a very great power toward recovery in all the acute diseases which are accompanied by fevers and are decided within forty days." For if indeed he considered easy breathing to have great power toward recovery, clearly he did not suppose that the forms of difficult breathing were always free of danger; so that the other forms of difficult breathing are just this alone, not without danger, whereas the one mentioned last of all, besides being dangerous and deadly, indicates that the person will die before long.

διὰ τοῦτ’ 930 οὖν οὐχ ἁπλῶς ὀλέθριον εἶναι αὐτὴν ἔφη, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὸ κάρτα προσέφερεν, καὶ οὐδὲ τούτῳ πάλιν ἀρκεσθεὶς ἐπήνεγκε τὸ ἤδη, ὡσὰν ὑπόγυιον τὸν θάνατον μόνης ταύτης τῆς δυσπνοίας ὑπαγορευούσης. οὕτως γὰρ ὀλέθριός ἐστιν, ὥστε μοι δοκεῖ, ὅτι οὐδ’ ἂν ἔγραψέ τι περὶ αὐτῆς ὁ Ἱπποκράτης, εἰ μὴ τὸν τρόπον ὅλον ὑπεξῄει, εἰπὼν περὶ τῶν δυσπνοιῶν. ἃ γὰρ ἀποθνήσκουσιν ἤδη γίγνεται, ταῦτ’ οὐδεμίαν ἔχει μεγάλην ἐν προγνώσεσιν ἀξίαν· ἀγαθῶν δ’ ἰατρῶν ἔργον, οὐ τὰ τοῖς ἰδιώταις ἤδη φαινόμενα προγινώσκειν, ἀλλ’ ὅσα τε τούτους λανθάνει καὶ μηδὲν φαῦλον ἔχειν δοκοῦντος τοῦ κάμνοντος φαίνεται. ἀλλὰ διὰ τὸ τὰ πάντ’ ἐφεξῆς ἐθελῆσαι γράψαι τὰ τῆς δυσπνοίας εἴδη δοκεῖ μοι καὶ ταύτης ἐπιμνησθῆναι, καὶ προσέτι διὰ τὴν τῆς μεγάλης τε καὶ πυκνῆς ἀντίθεσιν. ταυτὶ μὲν οὖν ἐνταῦθα τελευτάτω.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τὸν τρόπον ὅλον ὑπεξῄει

For this reason, 930 then, he did not say simply that it is deadly, but also added the 'exceedingly', and, not content even with this, he further added the 'already', on the grounds that this dyspnoea alone indicates that death is imminent. For it is so deadly that it seems to me that Hippocrates would not even have written anything about it, had he not been going through the whole subject in order while speaking about the dyspnoeas. For the things that occur to those who are already dying have no great value in prognoses; and it is the task of good physicians to foreknow, not the things already evident to laymen, but all those which escape them and which appear while the patient seems to have nothing bad about him. But because he wished to write down all the kinds of dyspnoea in sequence, it seems to me that he mentioned this one too, and moreover on account of the opposition to the large and frequent (breathing). Let these matters, then, end here.

For this reason, therefore, he did not say simply that it is deadly, but also added 'very', and, again not content even with this, he appended 'already', as though this difficulty of breathing alone indicates that death is imminent. For it is so deadly that it seems to me that Hippocrates would not have written anything about it at all, had he not been going through the whole manner [of the subject] in speaking about the difficulties of breathing. For the things that already occur in the dying have no great value in prognoses. The task of good physicians is not to foreknow what is already apparent to laymen, but whatever escapes their notice and appears while the patient seems to have nothing bad. But why did he write nothing in the Prognostic about uneven dyspnoea, even though he recognised it not only in the second and the sixth books of the Epidemics, 931 but also in the first and the third, and further in the Regimen in Acute Diseases and in the Aphorisms, and in many other places in his writings? For in the second and the sixth he mentioned it in the passage written out above, saying: 'a double recall inwards, as if they breathe in a second time.' And further, in the second book of the Epidemics, in narrating what happened to the woman Lycie, he added: 'and the breath was doubled.' For this differs in no way from 'a double recall inwards, as if they breathe in a second time.' And in the sixth he says: 'if the breath is left behind within (ἐγκαταλείπηται).' For this too is the same as what is said in the Regimen: 'breath stumbling in its upward movement.' For just as some people, when breathing in, do not do this in a single act, but interrupt the activity with a pause, like children sobbing, so too when breathing out. But also in the passages after the account of baths he has spoken of 'stretchings-forth (προτάσεις) of breaths,' indicating the interrupted activity.

But also in what follows the discussion of baths he has spoken of 'forward-stretchings of breaths', indicating the interrupted activity.

And in the first book of the Epidemics, in the case of Philiscus, he spoke in this manner: 'in this man 932 the breath throughout, as of one recalling it'; for here too 'recalling' is said in the same sense as 'double recall inwards'. Again, in the case of the wife of Dromeades, he said, 'breath rare, large, at once drawn up'; for this too has been said in the same way. And here indeed one may especially marvel at how each of the two dyspnoeas indicated the condition proper to it. For this woman was not only convulsed but also became delirious, inasmuch as she had not only the dyspnoea peculiar to convulsions but also that of delirium.

And in the first book of the Epidemics, in the case of Philiscus, he spoke in this manner: 'In this man 932 the breath throughout was as of one recalling [it].' ἐν δὲ τῷ φάσκειν· ὅταν θέρος γένηται ἦρι ὅμοιον, ἱδρῶτας ἐν τοῖσι πυρετοῖσι πολλοὺς προσδέχεσθαι· 934 δῆλός ἐστιν ἐπισημαινόμενος ὡς τοῦ προγνωστικοῦ μέρους τῆς τέχνης ἐστὶ τὰ τοιαῦτα. τὸ γὰρ προσδέχεσθαι καὶ προσδοκᾷν καὶ ἐλπίζειν, καὶ πάνθ’ ὅσα τοιαῦτα ῥήματα, προγνώσεως ἴδια. καὶ μὲν δὴ κᾀν τῷ φάσκειν· ἢν μὲν ὁ χειμὼν αὐχμηρὸς καὶ βόρειος γένηται, τὸ δ’ ἔαρ ἔπομβρον καὶ νότιον, ἀνάγκη τοῦ θέρεος πυρετοὺς ὀξέας καὶ ὀφθαλμίας καὶ δυσεντερίας γενέσθαι· σαφῶς ἐνδείκνυται ταὐτὸ τοῦτο, καὶ πολύ γε μᾶλλον, ὅταν ὡδί πως γράφῃ περὶ τῶν οὔρων· οἷσιν οὖρα παχέα, θρομβώδεα, ὀλίγα, οὐκ ἀπυρέτοισι, πλῆθος ἐλθὸν ἐκ τούτων λεπτὸν ὠφελεῖ· μάλιστα δὲ τὰ τοιαῦτα ἔρχεται, ἐν οἷσιν ἂν ἐξ ἀρχῆς διὰ ταχέων ὑπόστασιν ἴσχῃ. καὶ πάλιν· οἷσιν οὖρα ἀνατεταραγμένα οἷον ὑποζυγίου, τουτέοισι κεφαλαλγίαι ἢ πάρεισιν, ἢ παρέσονται. καὶ πάλιν· οἷσιν ἑβδομαῖα κρίνονται, ἐπινέφελον ἴσχει τῇ τετάρτῃ τὸ οὖρον ἐρυθρὸν, καὶ τἄλλα κατὰ λόγον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀνατεταραγμένα

and in saying: when the summer turns out like spring, expect many sweats in the fevers; 934 he is clearly indicating that such things belong to the prognostic part of the art. For 'to expect' and 'to anticipate' and 'to look for', and all verbs of this kind, are proper to prognosis. And indeed also in saying: if the winter is dry and northerly, and the spring rainy and southerly, it is necessary that in the summer acute fevers and ophthalmias and dysenteries arise; he clearly shows this same thing, and much more so when he writes somewhat as follows about urines: those in whom the urines are thick, clotted, and scanty, they not being free of fever, an abundance of thin urine coming after these is beneficial; and such urines come especially in those in whom from the beginning it quickly has a sediment. And again: those whose urines are stirred up turbid, like that of a beast of burden, in these headaches are either present or will be present. And again: in those who are judged on the seventh day, the urine has a red cloud on the fourth day, and the rest in proportion.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐξ ἀρχῆς διὰ ταχέων, πλῆθος ἐλθὸν ἐκ τούτων λεπτὸν, καὶ τἄλλα κατὰ λόγον

and in saying: 'When summer turns out like spring, expect copious sweats in fevers,' 934 it is evident that he is indicating that such things belong to the prognostic part of the art. For 'to expect', 'to anticipate' and 'to hope', and all verbs of this sort, are proper to prognosis. Moreover, in saying: 'If the winter turns out dry and northerly, and the spring rainy and southerly, in the summer acute fevers, ophthalmias and dysenteries must necessarily occur,' he plainly indicates this very same thing, and much more so when he writes somewhat as follows about the urines: 'In those who have thick, clotted, scanty urines, not without fever, a copious quantity of thin urine coming after these is beneficial; and such urines come chiefly in those who from the beginning, quickly, have a sediment.' And again: 'In those whose urines are stirred up, like that of a beast of burden, headaches are either present or will be present.' And again: 'In those who reach a crisis on the seventh day, the urine has a red cloud on the fourth day, and the other things accordingly.'

Greek (Kühn)

ὁμοίως δὲ τούτοις καὶ τὰ τῆς κύστεως καὶ τῶν νεφρῶν τὰς διαθέσεις δηλοῦντα κατὰ τοὺς ἀφορισμοὺς γέγραπται, παραλέλειπται δὲ ἐν τῷ προγνωστικῷ καὶ ἄλλα πάμπολλα, 935 καθάπερ καὶ τὰ τοιαῦτα· τῶν νοσημάτων ὁκόσοισιν ἀρχομένων χολὴ μέλαινα ἢ ἄνω ἢ κάτω ὑπέλθοι, θανάσιμον· δυσεντερίη ἢν ἀπὸ χολῆς μελαίνης ἄρξηται, θανάσιμον· οἷσιν ἐν πυρετοῖσιν αἱμοῤῥαγεῖ πλῆθος ὁκοθενοῦν, ἐν τοῖσιν ἀναλήψεσι τοῖσι τοιουτέοισιν αἱ κοιλίαι καθυγραίνονται. τοιαῦτά τε πάμπολλα κατὰ τοὺς ἀφορισμοὺς ἔστιν εὑρεῖν γεγραμμένα τοῦ προγνωστικοῦ μέρους τέχνης. τί δή ποτ’ οὖν οὐδὲν τούτων ἐν τῷ προγνωστικῷ γράμματι διῆλθεν; ὅτι περὶ τῶν ὀξέων νοσημάτων ἐν ἐκείνῳ προὔθετο διδάσκειν, ὡς ἀρχόμενός τε ἅμα καὶ συντελέσας τὸ βιβλίον, αὐτὸς ἡρμήνευσεν, ἐν ἀρχῇ μὲν εἰπών· σκέπτεσθαι δὲ χρὴ ὧδε ἐν τοῖσιν ὀξέσι νοσήμασιν· ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς τελευτῆς προσγράψας ὡδί· ταῦτα δὲ λέγω περὶ τῶν ὀξέων νοσημάτων, καὶ ὅσα ἐκ τούτων γίγνεται. τίνα δέ ἐστιν ἐκ τούτων γιγνόμενα, πάλιν αὐτὸς ὁ Ἱπποκράτης ἐν ταὐτῷ βιβλίῳ διέρχεται· οἱ δὲ ὕδρωπες οἱ ἐκ τῶν ὀξέων νοσημάτων πάντες κακοί.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Similarly to these, the things that indicate the conditions of the bladder and of the kidneys have also been written in the Aphorisms, but have been left out in the Prognostic, as have a very great many other things as well, 935 such as the following: 'In whichever diseases black bile passes off, either upward or downward, at their beginning, it is deadly'; 'dysentery, if it begins from black bile, is deadly'; 'in those who in fevers haemorrhage copiously from whatever source, during the recoveries of such persons the bowels become loose.' And a very great many such things, belonging to the prognostic part of the art, one can find written in the Aphorisms. Why, then, did he go through none of these in the treatise Prognostic? Because in that work he had proposed to teach about acute diseases, as he himself explained both when beginning and when completing the book, saying at the beginning: 'One must observe as follows in acute diseases'; and at the end adding this: 'I say these things concerning acute diseases, and all those that arise from them.' And which are the ones that arise from them Hippocrates himself again goes through in the same book: 'The dropsies that come from acute diseases are all bad.'

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὑπέλθοι, ἀναλήψεσι, καθυγραίνονται

In the same way as these, the things that reveal the conditions of the bladder and the kidneys are also written in the Aphorisms, while a great many other things have been omitted in the Prognostic, 935 such as the following: 'In whatever diseases black bile passes off, either upwards or downwards, at their beginning, it is fatal.' 'Dysentery, if it begins from black bile, is fatal.' 'In those who in fevers have a copious haemorrhage from any place whatever, in the recoveries of such persons the bowels become moist.' And a great many such things, belonging to the prognostic part of the art, can be found written in the Aphorisms. Why, then, did he go through none of these in the Prognostic treatise? Because in that work he set out to teach about acute diseases, as he himself explained both when beginning and when completing the book: at the beginning he said, 'One must examine thus in acute diseases'; and at the end he added the following: 'I say these things about acute diseases and those that arise from them.' What the things arising from these are, Hippocrates himself again goes through in the same book: 'The dropsies that arise from acute diseases are all bad.'

Greek (Kühn)

τοῦτο δὲ πάλιν· εὔπνοιαν δὲ χρὴ νομίζειν κάρτα μεγάλην δύναμιν ἐς σωτηρίαν ἔχειν ἐν πᾶσι τοῖσιν ὀξέσι 936 νοσήμασιν, ὁκόσα σὺν πυρετοῖσι καὶ ἐν τεσσαράκοντα ἡμέρῃσι κρίνεται. κᾀνταῦθα τῶν ἐκ μεταπτώσεως ὀξέων μνημονεύει, καθάπερ κᾀν τοῖς περὶ κρισίμων ἀπεδείξαμεν. ἐπεσημήνατο δὲ καὶ ἄλλοθεν πολλαχόθεν τοῦ συγγράμματος, ὡς ἤτοι περὶ τῶν ὀξέων νοσημάτων, ἢ περὶ τῶν γινομένων ἐκ τούτων ὁ λόγος ἐστὶν αὐτῷ, καθάπερ κᾀν τοῖσδε· οἱ δὲ ἱδρῶτες ἄριστοι μέν εἰσιν ἐν πᾶσι τοῖσιν ὀξέσι νοσήμασι, οἳ ἂν ἐν ἡμέρῃσι κρισίμοισι γένωνται. καὶ τί δεῖ πολλὰ λέγειν; αὐτὸς γὰρ, ὡς ἑκὼν παραλείπει πολλὰ τῶν προγνωστικῶν σημείων ἐν ἐκείνῳ τῷ βιβλίῳ, σαφῶς ἐνδείκνυται, ποτὲ μὲν ὡδί πως λέγων· μὴ ἐξαπατάτω δέ σε, ἤν τι αὐτέη ἡ κύστις νόσημα ἔχουσα, τῶν οὔρων τι ἀποδιδοῖ τουτέων, οὐ γὰρ τοῦ ὅλου σημεῖον, ἀλλ’ αὐτῆς καθ’ ἑαυτήν. οὐ μὴν προστίθησί γε τὰ γνωρίσματα κατὰ τὸ προγνωστικὸν, ἀλλ’ ἐν τοῖς ἀφορισμοῖς γράφει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And this again: 'One must consider that easy breathing has a very great power for safety in all acute 936 diseases which are accompanied by fevers and are judged within forty days.' Here too he makes mention of the acute diseases that arise from a shift, just as we also demonstrated in the books On Critical Days. He also indicated in many other places of the treatise that his discourse concerns either acute diseases or those which arise from them, as also in these words: 'Sweats are best in all acute diseases when they occur on critical days.' And why need one say much? For he himself clearly shows that he deliberately omits many of the prognostic signs in that book, at one point speaking somewhat as follows: 'But let it not deceive you if the bladder itself, having some disease, yields any of these urines; for this is not a sign of the whole, but of the bladder itself by itself.' Yet he does not add the distinguishing marks in the Prognostic, but writes them in the Aphorisms.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μεταπτώσεως, σωτηρίαν, γνωρίσματα

And again this: 'One must consider that easy breathing has a very great power towards survival in all acute 936 diseases that are accompanied by fevers and come to a crisis within forty days.' Here too he is referring to the acute diseases that arise by transition, as we have also shown in our work On Critical Days. In many other places of the treatise as well he has indicated that his discussion concerns either acute diseases or the diseases that arise from them, as in these words: 'Sweats are best in all acute diseases when they occur on critical days.' And why need one say much? For he himself clearly shows that he deliberately omits many of the prognostic signs in that book, at one point speaking roughly as follows: 'Let it not deceive you if the bladder itself, having some disease, passes any of these urines; for it is a sign not of the whole body, but of the bladder itself by itself.' Yet he does not add the distinguishing marks in the Prognostic, but writes them in the Aphorisms.

K7.936-937K. VII 936–937, ch. 3.9 difficulty high (3.05/100; thin 5, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) judge_failed_gate unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

αὖθις δ’ αὖ λέγει· χρὴ δὲ καὶ τὰς διαφορὰς τῶν νοσημάτων τῶν ἐπιδημεόντων ταχέων ἐνθυμέεσθαι καὶ μὴ λανθάνειν τῆς ὥρας τὴν κατάστασιν· οὐ μὴν οὐδὲ περὶ τούτου τι διδάσκων ἡμᾶς ἐν 937 προγνωστικῷ. προὔθετο γὰρ, ὡς ἔφην, τὰ τῶν ὀξέων νοσημάτων ἴδια διελθεῖν μόνα, τὰ δέ γε τῶν χρονίων ἴδια σύμπαντα παραλιπεῖν, ὥστ’ εὐλόγως καὶ τὴν ἀνώμαλον δύσπνοιαν, ἐξ ἑτέρου γένους ὑπάρχουσαν νοσήματος, ἐνταῦθα παρέλιπεν. εἰ γὰρ δὴ σπωμένων γίγνεται τῶν ἀναπνευστικῶν ὀργάνων, εἴη μὲν ἂν καὶ τοῖς χρονίοις νοσήμασι συνεδρεῦον τὸ πάθημα, καὶ ταύτῃ γε μηδὲν δὴ τῶν ὀξέων ἢ χρονίων ἴδιον· εἴη δ’ ἂν καὶ χωρὶς πυρετοῦ γιγνόμενον, ὥς γε καὶ οἱ σπασμοί· πρόκειται δ’ αὐτῷ κατὰ τὸ προγνωστικὸν ὑπὲρ τῶν ἰδίως ὀξέων ὀνομαζομένων ποιήσασθαι τὸν λόγον. ἔστι δὲ ταῦτα τὰ σὺν πυρετοῖς γιγνόμενα συνεχέσι τοὐπίπαν, ὡς αὐτὸς ἐν τῷ περὶ διαίτης ὀξέων ἐδήλωσεν ὡδί πως γράψας· ἔστι δὲ ἄρα ταῦτα ὀξέα, ὁποῖα ὠνόμασαν οἱ ἀρχαῖοι πλευρῖτιν καὶ περιπνευμονίαν καὶ φρενῖτιν καὶ καῦσον καὶ τἄλλα ὅσα τούτων ἐχόμενα, ὧν οἱ πυρετοὶ τοὐπίπαν ξυνεχέες.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And again in turn he says: "One must also quickly consider the differences of the diseases that are epidemic, and not let the constitution of the season escape notice"—yet he does not teach us anything about this either in the 937 Prognostic. For he set out, as I said, to go through only the features peculiar to acute diseases, and to leave aside all the features peculiar to chronic ones, so that with good reason he left out here the irregular dyspnoea as well, since it belongs to another genus of disease. For if indeed it arises when the respiratory organs are convulsed, the affection would also attend chronic diseases, and in this respect at least it would be peculiar to neither acute nor chronic diseases; and it would also occur without fever, just as convulsions too do. But his purpose in the Prognostic is to make his account about the diseases properly named acute. And these are the ones that occur, for the most part, with continuous fevers, as he himself showed in On Regimen in Acute Diseases, writing roughly as follows: "These, then, are acute: such as the ancients named pleuritis and peripneumonia and phrenitis and causus, and the others that are akin to these, whose fevers are for the most part continuous."

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ταχέων (Kühn's reading; Hippocratic tradition may have ταχέως, 'quickly', with ἐπιδημεόντων)

And again he says: 'One must also consider the differences of the swift diseases that are epidemic, and the constitution of the season must not escape notice'; yet he teaches us nothing about this either in the 937 Prognostic. For he set out, as I said, to go through only what is peculiar to acute diseases, and to leave aside everything peculiar to chronic ones. So it was reasonable that he also left aside here the irregular difficulty of breathing, since it belongs to another genus of disease. For if it does arise when the respiratory organs are convulsed, the affection would also accompany chronic diseases, and in this respect it would be peculiar to neither the acute nor the chronic. It would also occur without fever, as convulsions themselves do. But his purpose in the Prognostic is to discourse about the diseases properly called acute. These are the ones that occur with fevers that are, for the most part, continuous, as he himself made clear in On Regimen in Acute Diseases, writing more or less as follows: 'These, then, are acute: such as the ancients named pleuritis and peripneumonia and phrenitis and causus, and the others connected with these, in which the fevers are for the most part continuous.'

οὔκουν οἱ σπασμοὶ τούτου τοῦ γένους εἰσὶ τῶν νοσημάτων, ὅθεν οὐδὲ τὴν ἴδιον αὐτῶν δύσπνοιαν ἅμα ﻿ 938 τούτοις διῆλθε, καθάπερ οὐδὲ περὶ τῶν οὔρων, ἢ τῶν διαχωρημάτων, ἢ τῶν ἄλλων ἁπάντων, ὅσα κατὰ τοὺς ἀφορισμοὺς ἔγραψε, διότι τῶν ὀξέων νοσημάτων οὐκ ἦν ἴδια. διὰ τί δ’ οὐδὲν οὐδὲ περὶ τῶν τοιούτων δυσπνοιῶν ἐδίδαξεν ἐν τῷ προγνωστικῷ, ἐν αἷς ἤτοι μόνον τὸ εἰσπνεόμενον, ἢ μόνον τὸ ἐκπνεόμενόν ἐστι μέγα, περιττὸν ἡγοῦμαι νῦν ἐπεξιέναι, δείξας γε σαφῶς ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ τῶνδε τῶν ὑπομνημάτων, ὡς εἰς πέντε ταύτας τὰς δυσπνοίας, ὑπὲρ ὦν νῦν πέπαυμαι λέγων, αὔταρκες ἀνάγειν τὰς ἄλλας διαφοράς· οὐδὲ γὰρ ἢ ταχείας, ἢ βραδείας ἀναπνοῆς ἐμνημόνευσεν ἐν προγνωστικῷ. κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν δὲ λόγον ἐδείξαμεν, ὡς πάσας τὰς ἄλλας διαφορὰς ἐν ταῖς προειρημέναις περιέχεσθαι συμβέβηκε, ὥσθ’ ἑκάτερον αὐτῶν ὀρθῶς ὁ Ἱπποκράτης φαίνεται ποιήσας, ἐν μὲν τοῖς τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν τὴν εἰς ἁπάσας τὰς κατὰ μέρος δυσπνοίας διαίρεσιν τιθέμενος, ἐν δὲ τῷ προγνωστικῷ καὶ τοῖς ἀφορισμοῖς τὸ πρὸς τὴν χρείαν ἐπεῖγον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Convulsions, then, are not of this class of diseases, which is why he did not go through the dyspnoea peculiar to them together with 938 these, just as he did not go through the urines, or the stools, or all the other things that he wrote about in the Aphorisms, because they were not peculiar to the acute diseases. But why he also taught nothing in the Prognostic about those dyspnoeas in which either only what is inhaled or only what is exhaled is large, I consider it superfluous to go over now, since I showed clearly in the first of these commentaries that it is sufficient to refer the other differences to these five dyspnoeas, about which I have just now finished speaking; for neither did he mention quick or slow respiration in the Prognostic. And by the same argument we showed that all the other differences happen to be contained in those previously mentioned, so that Hippocrates appears to have done each of the two correctly: in the books of the Epidemics setting out the division into all the particular dyspnoeas, and in the Prognostic and the Aphorisms what is pressing for practical use.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπεῖγον, ὑπομνημάτων

Convulsions, then, do not belong to this class of diseases; and for this reason he did not go through the difficulty of breathing peculiar to them together 938 with these, just as he did not go through urines either, or stools, or any of the other things that he wrote about in the Aphorisms, because they were not peculiar to acute diseases. As for why he taught nothing in the Prognostic about such difficulties of breathing either, those in which either only the inspiration or only the expiration is large, I think it superfluous to go into it now. I showed clearly in the first of these commentaries that it is sufficient to refer the other differences back to these five difficulties of breathing, about which I have just finished speaking. Nor, indeed, did he make mention of either rapid or slow breathing in the Prognostic. By the same reasoning we showed that all the other differences turn out to be contained in those previously mentioned. Hippocrates, then, evidently did each of these things correctly: in the books of the Epidemics he set out the division into all the particular difficulties of breathing, and in the Prognostic and the Aphorisms he set out what is pressing for practical use.

939 Ὁπότ’ οὖν ταῦθ’ ἡμῖν ἱκανῶς ἀποδέδεικται, λείποιτ’ ἂν ἔτι τὰς ὑπολοίπους ἐξ ἁπάντων αὐτῶν τῶν συγγραμμάτων ἐξηγήσασθαι ῥήσεις, ἀρξαμένους αὖθις ἀπὸ τοῦ δευτέρου τε καὶ ἕκτου τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν. ἐν ἀμφοτέροις γὰρ τούτοις τοῖς βιβλίοις, ἐφεξῆς τῇ πρώτῃ πασῶν εἰρημένῃ ῥήσει τῇ διδασκούσῃ τὰς διαφορὰς ἁπάσης τῆς δυσπνοίας, ὡδί πως γέγραπται· ἰητήριον συνεχέων χασμέων μακρόπνους· ἐν τοῖσιν ἀπότοισι καὶ μόγις πίνουσι, βραχύπνους. ἐνταῦθ’ εἴτε τὸν ἄνθρωπον μακρόπνουν, εἴτε τὴν δύσπνοιαν αὐτὴν οὕτως ὠνόμασεν, ὅτι τὸ διὰ μακροῦ χρόνου γινόμενον, τουτέστι τὸ ἀραιὸν πνεῦμα καὶ προσέτι τὸ πολὺ, δηλοῦται διὰ τῆς προσηγορίας ταύτης, ἐν τῷ δευτέρῳ δέδεικται λόγῳ, καθάπερ γε καὶ ὡς τὸ δι’ ὀλίγου, τοῦτ’ ἔστι τὸ πυκνὸν, καὶ προσέτι τὸ μικρὸν, ἐκ τοῦ, βραχύπνους.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

939 Since, then, these things have been sufficiently demonstrated by us, it would still remain to expound the remaining passages from all his writings, beginning again from the second and the sixth books of the Epidemics. For in both these books, next after the passage stated first of all, the one that teaches the differences of dyspnoea as a whole, it is written somewhat as follows: 'Curative of continuous yawnings, long-breathed; in those who do not drink and drink with difficulty, short-breathed.' Here, whether he so named the man long-breathed, or the dyspnoea itself, that the breath which comes about over a long time, that is, the rare breath and in addition the large, is signified by this appellation, has been shown in the second book, just as also that the breath which comes about at a short interval, that is, the frequent, and in addition the small, is signified by 'short-breathed'.

939 Since, then, these things have been sufficiently demonstrated by us, it would still remain to explain the remaining passages from all of his writings, beginning again from the second and the sixth books of the Epidemics. Here, whether he so named the person long-breathed or the difficulty of breathing itself, it has been shown in the second book that what occurs at a long interval of time, that is, sparse breath and moreover copious breath, is indicated by this designation; and likewise that what occurs at a short interval, that is, frequent breath and moreover small breath, is indicated by 'short-breathed'.

Greek (Kühn)

ἰητήριον οὖν ἤτοι σημεῖον ἢ αἴτιον, ἑκάτερον γὰρ ἔστι δείξασθαι, τῶν συνεχῶν χασμῶν, τὸ μέγα καὶ ἀραιὸν γίγνεται (τὸ) πνεῦμα· σημεῖον μὲν, ὡς εἰς τἀναντία τῆς διαθέσεως μεθισταμένης, αἴτιον δὲ, ὅτι ἐναντίων ἐναντία 940 τὰ ἰάματα· χασμῶνται γὰρ καὶ σκορδινῶνται συνεχῶς, ἀτμώδους τινὸς καὶ παχέος πνεύματος ἐν τῷ μυώδει γένει κατειλημμένου, καθότι δέδεικται δι’ ἑτέρων· εὐθὺς δ’ οἷς τοῦτο συμβέβηκε καὶ συνεχῶς ἐκπνέουσιν, ὡς ἂν ἐκκαθαιρομένου τε καὶ κενουμένου τοῦ τοιούτου πνεύματος ἐν ταῖς ἐκπνοαῖς· εἰ δέ τις μηκέτι συνεχῶς, ἀλλ’ ἤδη διὰ μακροῦ χρόνου τὰς ἐκπνοὰς ποιεῖται, δῆλον ὡς αἱ προϋπάρχουσαι διαθέσεις αὐτῷ πέπαυνται καὶ σημεῖον οὕτως ἰητήριον ἡ μακρόπνοια τῆς χάσμης ἔσται. καὶ μὲν δὴ καὶ ὡς αἰτίαν ἰωμένην τὴν μακρόπνοιαν τὰς χάσμας τὰς συνεχεῖς ἀκούειν ἐγχωρεῖ, δοκεῖ μοι καὶ αὐτὸ μηκέτι δεῖσθαι μακροῦ λόγου. τὸ γὰρ ἕως πλείστου τὴν ἀναπνοὴν ἐπισχεῖν, ὅπερ ὀνομάζεται κατάληψις πνεύματος, ᾧ λόγῳ τοὺς λυγμοὺς ἰᾶται, τούτῳ καὶ τὰς χάσμας ἰάσεται. καὶ γὰρ οὖν καὶ ποιοῦσιν ἐπὶ τοῖς γυμνασίοις αὐτὸ σύμπαντες οἱ ἀθληταὶ, διώσασθαι τῇ βίᾳ σπεύδοντες ἅπαν ὅσον ἐν τοῖς κατὰ δέρμα λεπτοῖς πόροις, ἢ ὑγρῶν ἢ ἀτμῶν ἴσχεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μυώδει γένει

Curative, then, of continuous yawnings—whether as a sign or as a cause, for either can be shown—is the breath that becomes great and rare: a sign, inasmuch as the condition is shifting over into its opposites; a cause, because the remedies of opposites are opposites. 940 For people yawn and stretch continuously when some vaporous and thick pneuma has been trapped in the muscular kind, as has been shown elsewhere; and those to whom this has happened straightaway also breathe out continuously, since such pneuma is being cleared out and evacuated in the expirations. But if someone no longer makes his expirations continuously, but now at a long interval, it is clear that the conditions previously present in him have ceased, and in this way long-breathing will be a curative sign of the yawning. And indeed that it is also possible to understand long-breathing as a cause curing continuous yawnings—this too seems to me no longer to require a long account. For holding the respiration for as long as possible, which is called retention of breath, will cure yawnings by the same reasoning by which it cures hiccups. And in fact all athletes also do this after their exercises, striving to force out by violence everything, whether of moistures or of vapours, that is held in the fine pores of the skin.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: (τὸ), τῷ μυώδει γένει, εὐθὺς

Healing, then, whether as sign or as cause (for it is possible to demonstrate each), of continuous yawnings is the large and rare breathing: a sign, inasmuch as the condition is changing over into its opposite; a cause, because the remedies of opposites are opposites. 940 For people yawn and stretch continuously when some vaporous and thick pneuma is trapped in the muscular kind of tissue, as has been shown elsewhere; and those to whom this has happened at once also breathe out continuously, as though such pneuma were being cleared out and evacuated in the expirations. But if someone no longer makes his expirations continuously, but now at long intervals of time, it is clear that the conditions previously present in him have ceased, and thus long breathing will be a healing sign of the yawning. Moreover, that it is also possible to understand long breathing as a cause that cures the continuous yawnings, this too, it seems to me, no longer needs a long account. For holding back respiration for as long as possible, which is called retention of breath, will cure yawnings on the same principle by which it cures hiccups. And indeed all athletes do this after their exercises, striving to force out by violence everything that is held in the fine pores in the skin, whether of moistures or of vapours.

Greek (Kühn)

διὰ ταῦτα μὲν οὖν ἡ μακρόπνοια τῶν συνεχῶν χασμῶν σημεῖόν ἐστιν, ἢ αἴτιον 941 ἰατρικόν· ἐν δ’ αὖ τοῖσιν ἀπότοισι καὶ μόγις πίνουσιν ἡ βραχύπνοια· τοῦτο δ’ οὐκέτι σαφῶς, ἀλλ’ ἤδη συμβολικώτερον εἴρηται. πότερον γὰρ τοὺς φρενιτικοὺς ἡγητέον ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ δηλοῦσθαι νῦν, ὅτι καὶ δι’ ἄλλων εἶπεν, οἱ φρενιτικοὶ βραχυπόται, ψόφου καθαπτόμενοι, τρομώδεες; ἢ μᾶλλον οἷς κατέψυκται τὰ περὶ τὸν πνεύμονα καὶ τὴν καρδίαν, ὡς ἀναπνεῖν τε ἅμα διὰ μακροῦ καὶ ψυχρὸν ἐκπνεῖν; εἰσαγομένη γὰρ ἐξ ἀμφοῖν ἡ βραχύπνοια, σημεῖον ἰητήριον· ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν κατέψυγμένων οὐ σημεῖον μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ αἴτιον. ὡς γὰρ ἡ κατάληψις τοῦ πνεύματος ἐκκενοῦν ἠδύνατο τὰ πε ριττὰ καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ταύτην ἐπὶ τῶν λυζόντων τε καὶ χασμωμένων πολλὰ καὶ σκορδινωμένων καὶ συντόνως γεγυμνασμένων παρελαμβάνομεν, οὕτως ἡ συνεχὴς ἀναπνοὴ ῥιπίσαι τε ἅμα καὶ ἐκθερμᾶναι τὰ κατεψυγμένα δυνήσεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀπότοισι, καθαπτόμενοι, εἰσαγομένη ἐξ ἀμφοῖν

For these reasons, then, long-breathing is a sign, or a curative cause, of continuous yawnings; 941 and again, in those who are without thirst for drink and drink only with difficulty, short-breathing is so. But this is no longer stated clearly, but now rather more enigmatically. For should one suppose that the phrenitics are indicated by it here, because he also said elsewhere, "phrenitics are small drinkers, affected by noise, tremulous"? Or rather those in whom the parts about the lung and the heart have been chilled, so that they both breathe at long intervals and breathe out cold air? For short-breathing, when it comes to be present in both cases, is a curative sign; but in those who have been chilled it is not only a sign but also a cause. For just as the holding of the breath was able to evacuate the superfluities, and on this account we employed it in those who hiccup and yawn much and stretch themselves and have exercised strenuously, so continuous respiration will be able at once both to fan and to heat thoroughly the parts that have been chilled.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀπότοισι, ψόφου καθαπτόμενοι, εἰσαγομένη, συμβολικώτερον, ἰατρικόν

For these reasons, then, long breathing is a sign, or a healing cause, of continuous yawnings. 'And again, in those who do not drink and drink with difficulty, short breathing': but this is no longer said clearly, but now rather more enigmatically. For are we to consider that he now means the phrenitic, because elsewhere too he said: 'the phrenitic are short drinkers, catching at noise, tremulous'? Or rather those in whom the parts about the lung and the heart have been chilled, so that they at once breathe in at long intervals and breathe out cold [breath]? For short breathing, when it is introduced in both, is a healing sign; but in the chilled it is not only a sign but also a cause. κατὰ τοῦτον οὖν τὸν λόγον κᾀν τῷ δευτέρῳ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν συνεβούλευσεν ἐπιτηδεύειν ὀξυθυμίην ἐμποιέειν, καὶ χροιῆς ἀναλήψιος ἕνεκα καὶ ἐκχυμώσιος, ὡς καὶ τῆς ὀξυθυμίας ἐπεγείρειν τὴν ἔμφυτον 942 θερμασίαν δυναμένης. ἀλλ’ ὅτι γε πυκνοτέραν ἐργάζεται πάντως ἡ ὀξυθυμία τὴν ἀναπνοὴν, οὐδεὶς ἀγνοεῖ. παραληψόμεθα οὖν αὐτὴν ἐπὶ τῶν κατεψυγμένων τε καὶ ἀχροούντων καὶ ἰσχνῶν, ἐκ διαλειμμάτων τέ τινων καὶ προσηκόντων καιρῶν· οὐ γὰρ δὴ συνεχῶς γε οὕτως, ὡς ἄγαν βλάψαι τὴν δύναμιν, ἀλλ’ ὥσπερ τὰ γυμνάσια τὰ ῥωννύντα τὴν ἔμφυτον θερμασίαν, οὐκ ἄμετρα ποιεῖσθαι κελεύομεν, ἀλλ’ ἐν καιρῷ τε καὶ μέτρῳ παραλαμβάνομεν, οὕτω καὶ βραχύπνοιαν, εἴ τε κατὰ μόνας εἴ τε καὶ μετὰ τῆς ὀξυθυμίας ἐπιτηδεύσομεν, οὐκ ἄνευ καιροῦ καὶ μέτρου παραληψόμεθα· προσέχειν δ’ ἐνταῦθα χρὴ τῷ λόγῳ καὶ μὴ νομίζειν τὴν αὐτὴν εἶναι διάθεσιν τῇ νῦν ὑφ’ ἡμῶν δηλουμένῃ τὴν κατὰ τὸ προγνωστικὸν, ἐφ’ ἧς ἔλεγε· ψυχρὸν δ’ ἐκπνεόμενον ἐκ τῶν ῥινῶν καὶ τοῦ στόματος, ὀλέθριον κάρτα ἤδη γίγνεται.

On this reasoning, then, in the second book of the Epidemics too he advised making it a practice to induce sharpness of temper, both for the sake of the recovery of complexion and for the sake of the diffusion of the humours (ἐκχύμωσις), since sharpness of temper too is able to rouse the innate 942 heat. But that sharpness of temper in any case makes respiration more frequent, no one is unaware. We shall therefore employ it in those who are chilled and ill-coloured and lean, at certain intermissions and at suitable occasions; for certainly not continuously, in such a way as to harm the faculty excessively, but just as we do not bid that the exercises which strengthen the innate heat be performed without measure, but employ them with due occasion and measure, so too short-breathing, whether we practise it on its own or together with sharpness of temper, we shall employ not without due occasion and measure. Here one must attend to the argument and not suppose that the condition in the Prognostic, concerning which he said, is the same as that which we are now indicating: "but cold breath exhaled from the nostrils and the mouth is by then very deadly."

For in those in acute diseases, about whom he makes his discussion in that book, dyspnoea of this kind indicates death; but in the other diseases, all those without fever, coldness takes hold of the whole habit of the 943 animal; it does not occur in these either without danger, yet not in every case destructively. For in all such conditions the innate heat is weighed down by an abundance of cold matter, in the manner of a flame being choked by much wet wood, so that it is even possible at some point to rekindle it, once one has gained mastery over the matter; in those cases, however, in which, the matter having been consumed, the heat itself is of necessity extinguished together with it, as in those in acute diseases who breathe out in this way, no hope of safety is left.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

One must therefore keep these things in mind, and also that in the apnoeas it is neither possible to suppose that the whole respiration has been completely lost, nor does what happens appear so to those who examine it carefully; rather, their breath becomes very small and at the same time occurs at very long intervals, and in this way it escapes the notice of most people. But if one applies one's 944 mind carefully, or even places one of one's fingers before the patient's nose, one will perceive the respiration. And if one also hangs a hair before it, or a fine piece of wool, thoroughly carded out, one will see these too being stirred. In such conditions, then, short-breathing, that is, the movement of the respiratory organs at short intervals of time, which indeed we also call frequent breath, is at once a cause and a remedial sign; and in the same way, in the case of continuous yawnings, not only the breathing at long intervals, that is, the rare one, is at once a remedial sign and cause, but also the large one. For this too is called, in a secondary sense, long-breathing. For primarily the breathing that occurs at a long interval of time, that is, the rare one, possesses this name, and secondarily the large one as well. This, then, is how matters stand regarding these. Let us now set out as well all the other things that have been written by him concerning difficulty of breathing.

In the second book of the Epidemics, then, in the case of the wife of Antigenes, having first said that she became asthmatic before childbirth, he adds after this: "then 945 together with the delivery she vomited up pus." So that this too agrees with what has been demonstrated by us; for those are asthmatic who breathe frequently and at the same time dilate the thorax to a great extent. This occurs sometimes in conditions that require much inspiration. At any rate, such a thing befalls those who have run or have exercised vigorously in any other way whatsoever, and all who have burning fevers. It also occurs, as was shown, when there is an obstruction, or compression, or some narrowing in the respiratory organs; and something of this sort appears to have happened also to the wife of Antigenes, as the pus made clear. This, then, is the first dyspnoea he mentions in the second book of the Epidemics; and after this, the one that befell Lycie, concerning whom, as I also said earlier, he wrote: "the breath was doubled, yet not great in proportion." For the nervous kind of her organs had been affected, so that a complete retention of the urine also occurred. For this reason, then, the doubled breathing came about; but why was it not great? Or was it because she had been chilled?

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κειμένη, περιέσταλτο

At any rate, he says, "she was contracted"; and he was shown to use this 946 word of those whose innate heat is being chilled away. Next, discussing also the cases of cynanche that occurred at that time, he writes as follows: "and the breath in these was not very elevated"—evidently on the ground that in those with cynanche the breath is for the most part elevated. It is his custom to call by this name the small breath, which, as it might seem to someone, enters only about as far as the pharynx and does not descend into the depth of the thorax. That the breath must necessarily be of this kind in those who suffer pain in any one whatever of the respiratory organs has been stated earlier. What, then, is the cause on account of which in those with cynanche the breath was not very elevated? Is it because the form of cynanche also did not occur in them in the accustomed manner? For first he will say: "the pharynx not inflamed, but lying [flat]" (κειμένη); then he says thus: "most of them not being choked, unless they attempted to swallow"; and in general the whole account of the cynanche that occurred at that time showed that the pharynx remained unaffected, while the parts neighbouring it, those continuous with the vertebrae of the neck, were affected.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: περιέσταλτο, τούτω, κειμένη, ἐρεῖ

At any rate he says, 'she was contracted'; and it has been shown 946 that he uses this word of those whose innate heat is being chilled. Next, discussing also the patients with cynanche who occurred at that time, he writes as follows: 'In these the breathing was not very elevated', implying, evidently, that in patients with cynanche the breathing is for the most part elevated. It is his custom to call by this name the small breathing, the kind that, as it might seem to someone, enters only somewhere thereabouts, as far as the pharynx, and does not descend into the depth of the thorax. That the breathing must be of this kind in those who feel pain in any of the respiratory organs whatsoever has been said before. What, then, is the cause why in these patients with cynanche the breathing was not very elevated? Is it because the form of the cynanche did not arise in them in the usual way either? For first he says: 'the pharynx not inflamed, but lying still'; then he says this: 'most of them not choking, unless they tried to swallow'. And in general the whole account of the cynanche that occurred at that time showed that the pharynx remained unaffected, whereas the parts neighbouring it, those continuous with the vertebrae of the neck, were affected.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μετέωρον, ὀλίγον

For indeed he says that the vertebrae themselves were also displaced, 947 some only inward, others also toward the sides. Since, then, no other of the respiratory organs was affected, nor indeed the pharynx itself in its own right, except in so far as it shared in the affections through the community of its position, it was fitting that their breath was not very elevated; for if the pharynx had not been troubled at all, it would not have been elevated in any way whatsoever; but since it was troubled by suffering in sympathy with the adjacent parts, the breath acquired the condition intermediate between those mentioned, being moderately frequent. After this constitution he mentions another which occurred in Perinthus, beginning his account in this manner: "We came to Perinthus a little about the summer solstice." Then he describes the whole form of the diseases, which began from causus, became vomitous, and persisted for a very long time. But he also added this, that the causus fevers occurred in these patients without vomiting, and that their bowels passed a great deal.

So that from these and from all the other things it is quite evident that the basis of the constitution had come about in phlegms undergoing putrefaction, the respiratory 948 organs having remained unaffected, and also the mouth of the stomach, while the causus set out once more from the lower parts. So that from these and from all the other things it is clearly evident that the basis of the constitution lay in putrefying phlegms, the respiratory organs 948 remaining unaffected, and also the mouth of the stomach, while the causus in turn arose from the lower parts. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

949 This patient too, then, clearly bears witness to what has often been said by us before; for since the hypochondria are joined to the respiratory organs, for this reason, when these were in pain, the breathing was somewhat frequent. These, then, are the kinds of dyspnoea he wrote of in the second book of the Epidemics. Concerning the latter, then, there would be no question at all; but concerning the one in Cranon, the wife of Nicostratus, he seems to me to have added the small and frequent breathing for this reason, because she became delirious. For it has already been said many times that, on the contrary, rare and large breathing is proper to delirium; so that it is worth inquiring why this did not occur, but rather its opposite, that which is proper to pains, the small and frequent.
