οὐδὲ γὰρ προσέθηκεν ἐν τῇ διηγήσει τῆς ἀῤῥωστίας, γενέσθαι τινὰ ὀδύνην οὐδενὸς τῶν μορίων, ἀλλ’ οὐδ’ ὄγκον 950 ἐπίσημον οὔτε καθ’ ὑποχόνδριον οὔτε κατὰ θώρακα. τί δὴ οὖν ἐστι τὸ γινόμενον; ἐμοὶ μὲν δοκεῖ διττή τις ἰδέα νοσήματος συστῆναι περὶ τὸ γύναιον ἀπὸ διττῆς ἀρχῆς· ἐν μὲν γὰρ τῷ παραφρονῆσαι καὶ τὸν τράχηλον καὶ τὸ στόμα καὶ τὴν ῥῖνα παρασπασθῆναι δῆλον ὡς ἐν τῇ κεφαλῇ τις ὑπόθεσις αὐτῇ νοσήματος ὑπῆρχεν· ἐκ δὲ τοῦ τὸν σῖτόν τε ἐγκατακλεισθῆναι μέχρι δεκάτης ἡμέρας καὶ τοῖς οὔροις ὀροβοειδῆ γενέσθαι τὴν ὑπόστασιν, οὐ μικρὰν ἐν ἥπατι νομίζω γενέσθαι φλεγμονὴν, ἐφ’ ἧς τὴν ἄνωθεν διάθεσιν νικηθεῖσαν οὐ δυνηθῆναι καθ’ ἑαυτὴν τυπῶσαι τὴν ἀναπνοήν. εἴρηται γὰρ οὖν καὶ πρόσθεν ἤδη πολλάκις, ὡς κατὰ τὴν ἐπικρατοῦσαν διάθεσιν ἡ τῆς δυσπνοίας ἰδέα σχηματίζεται. δηλοῖ δ’ οὐχ ἥκιστα τῆς παραφροσύνης τὸ βραχὺ καὶ ἡ λέξις αὐτὴ τοῦ παλαιοῦ. παραλέγειν γοῦν αὐτὴν εἶπεν· εἴωθεν δὲ τῷ τοιούτῳ ῥήματι χρῆσθαι μικρὰν ἐνδείξασθαι βλάβην βουλόμενος, ὡς καὶ τοῦτ’ ἀποδέδεικται πολλάκις.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὀροβοειδῆ, παραλέγειν

For neither did he add, in the account of the illness, that any pain occurred in any of the parts, nor indeed any notable swelling 950 either in the hypochondrium or in the thorax. What, then, is it that happened? To me it seems that a certain twofold form of disease was constituted in the woman from a twofold origin; for from her being delirious and from the neck and the mouth and the nose being drawn aside, it is clear that some basis of disease was present for her in the head; but from the fact that the food was shut up within until the tenth day and that the sediment in the urines became vetch-like (ὀροβοειδῆ), I think that no small inflammation arose in the liver, in consequence of which the condition from above, having been overcome, was not able to stamp the respiration with its own form. For indeed it has already been said many times before as well, that the form of the dyspnoea is shaped according to the prevailing condition. And the very wording of the ancient also shows, not least, the slightness of the delirium: at any rate, he said that she 'talked aside' (παραλέγειν); and he is accustomed to use such a word when he wishes to indicate a slight injury, as this too has been demonstrated many times.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐφ’ ἧς, τυπῶσαι, παραλέγειν, ὑπόθεσις, ὀροβοειδῆ

For in his account of the illness he did not add that any pain arose in any of the parts, nor any notable swelling 950 either in the hypochondrium or in the chest. What, then, is it that took place? It seems to me that a twofold form of disease was established in the woman from a twofold origin. From her being delirious and having her neck, mouth and nose drawn aside by spasm, it is clear that she had some basis of disease in the head. But from the food being shut in until the tenth day, and from the sediment in the urine becoming like bitter vetch, I think that no small inflammation arose in the liver. Under this inflammation the condition from above was overcome and could not by itself give its stamp to the respiration. For it has already been said many times before that the form of the difficulty of breathing is shaped according to the prevailing condition. Not least, the ancient's own wording shows that the delirium was slight. At any rate he said that she 'talked somewhat at random' (παραλέγειν). He is accustomed to use such a verb when he wishes to indicate a slight impairment, as this too has been demonstrated many times.

Τί δ’ ὅταν εἴπῃ, πνευματώδη τινὰ γενέσθαι, χρὴ δέχεσθαι; πότερον ὃν ἐν τῷ περὶ διαίτης ὀξέων πνευματίαν ἐκάλεσεν; ἢ τὸν πεπνευματωμένον τὴν γαστέρα; τί δέ ἐστιν ὁ πνευματίας, ἐν τῷ περὶ διαίτης ὀξέων αὐτὸς ἐξηγήσατο σαφέστατα. προειπὼν γὰρ, ὡς ταχέως πνευματίαι γίνονται, συνάπτων εὐθὺς ἐπιφέρει· ὑπὸ δὲ πολλοῦ καὶ πυκνοῦ πνεύματος. τοῦτο μὲν δὴ καὶ κατ’ ἀρχὰς εὐθὺς ἐξηγησάμην ἐν τῷδε τῷ βιβλίῳ· τὸ δ’ ἐν τῷ τετάρτῳ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν ἐπισκεπτέον, αὐτὴν ὑπογράψαντας τὴν ῥῆσιν ἔχουσαν ὧδε· ὁ ἐκ τῶν μετάλλων· ὑποχόνδριον δεξιὸν ἐντεταμένον, σπλὴν μέ γας, κοιλίη ἐντεταμένη, ὑπόσκληρος, πνευματώδης, ἄχροος. ἐγχωρεῖ γὰρ ἑκάτερον, καὶ δύσπνουν αὐτὸν γεγονέναι, διὰ (τε) τὰς ἐν τοῖς ὑποχονδρίοις διαθέσεις, καὶ πνευματωθῆναι τὴν γαστέρα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πνευματώδη, πνευματώδης, πνευματίαν, ὑπογράψαντας

It is therefore nothing to wonder at that, since she was delirious only to a slight degree but strongly inflamed in the liver, not 951 the dyspnoea proper to the delirium but that proper to the inflammation occurred. So, then, it stands with these matters as well. But when he says that someone became full of pneuma (pneumatōdēs), how ought one to take it? As the man whom in the Regimen in Acute Diseases he called pneumatias? Or as the man whose belly has been inflated with pneuma? What the pneumatias is, he himself explained most clearly in the Regimen in Acute Diseases. For having first said that they quickly become pneumatiai, he immediately adds in continuation: 'by much and frequent breath (pneuma)'. This indeed I explained right at the beginning in this book; but the passage in the fourth book of the Epidemics must be examined, once we have set down the statement itself, which runs as follows: 'The man from the mines: right hypochondrium tense, spleen large, belly tense, somewhat hard, full of pneuma, colourless.' For either is possible: both that he became dyspnoeic on account of the conditions in the hypochondria, and that his belly became inflated with pneuma.

It is therefore nothing to wonder at that, since she was delirious only briefly but was strongly inflamed in the liver, the difficulty of breathing that befell her was not 951 the one proper to the delirium but the one proper to the inflammation. But the passage in the fourth book of the Epidemics must be examined, once we have written out the text itself, which runs thus: 'The man from the mines: right hypochondrium distended, spleen large, belly distended, somewhat hard, full of pneuma, colourless.' 952 Much more unclear still is the next patient, who runs thus: "The son of Timaenes had something of pneuma, so as to be yellowish." For in this man too there was quite a serious malfunction of the liver, on account of which it was possible both that he had difficulty of breathing and that his belly was distended with wind; for the yellowish colour is a mark of a malfunctioning liver. But what is said next, thus: "The husband of the woman in childbed, the one at the house of Sitodocus, jaundiced, whom I visited on the seventh day, died on the eighth, neither passing urine nor passing stool; hypochondria large and hard, and breathing frequent"—this contains no problem at all. For when the hypochondria had become hard to no moderate degree and large, it is reasonable that his breathing was frequent. But wholly unclear is what follows on from this, said of the woman in childbed thus: "as the fever was lifting (διαίροντος), the breathing was not resolved." For what kind of difficulty of breathing she had, he neither himself showed clearly, nor did he write any other symptom of such a sort from which one might infer the condition that had come about in her.

And moreover, after this woman, having gone on not a little way, 953 he begins to narrate in the following manner: 'The wife of Timaenes, pneumatic (πνευματώδης).' For this can be understood as dyspnoeic, and it can also be taken, as most of the commentators think, as meaning that she had her hypochondria filled with pneuma; but it seems to me better, and perhaps even necessary, that it be the woman whose hypochondria were inflated with pneuma who is meant, rather than a dyspnoeic one. Then, having gone on a little in the account, he says, 'and in some degree breathing more easily,' as though he had already said beforehand of her that she was dyspnoeic. And that he is accustomed to call 'pneumatic' those whose breathing is frequent has been stated in what precedes, but it is clear now as well; for the symptoms of this woman turned once more for the worse, and he says that she died on the sixteenth day with her breathing frequent.

Greek (Kühn)

καὶ ὁ μετὰ ταύτην δὲ γεγραμμένος, ὅτι καὶ αὐτὸς ἀνέπνευσε πυκνὸν ἐπὶ τῇ τῶν ὑποχονδρίων διαθέσει, δῆλον ἡ ῥῆσις αὐτὴ ποιήσει, τόνδε τὸν τρόπον ἔχουσα· ὁ παῖς, ὃς ἦν τῆς γυναικὸς τῆς τοῦ Ἀποιμάντου ἀδελφῆς, ὑποχόνδρια 954 μεγάλα, καὶ σπλὴν, πνεῦμα, διαχώρησις πικρόχολος. ὥσπερ γὰρ πνευματώδεις ἐνίοτε καλεῖ τοὺς πυκνὸν ἀναπνέοντας, οὕτω ποτὲ καὶ ἁπλῶς γράφει, πνεῦμα. θαυμαστὸν δ’ οὐδὲν εἰ μεγάλων ὄντων ὑποχονδρίων, ἐγίγνετο πυκνὸν τὸ πνεῦμα. καὶ γὰρ αὖ καὶ προελθὼν ἐπ’ αὐτοῦ πάλιν ἐρεῖ· τὰ ὑποχόνδρια δὲ ἐντεταμένα ἦν, καὶ δὴ καὶ ἦν τι βηχίον ξηρὸν ἡσυχῆ. καθόλου γὰρ, ὡς ἔφαμεν, ἐπειδὰν ἢ ὄγκος, ἢ ὀδύνη τις ἐν τοῖς καθ’ ὑποχόνδρια συσταίη, μικρὸν καὶ πυκνὸν ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἀναπνέουσιν. ὥστ’ οὐδὲν θαυμαστὸν οὐδ’ ἐπειδὰν ἐν τοῖς μετὰ ταῦτα, προειπὼν, ὑποχόνδριον ὑπολάπαρον, προσθῇ, πνευματῶδες δὲ οὐ κάρτα. εἰ γὰρ δὴ τὸ μὲν λαπαρὸν κενὸν, τὸ δὲ ὑπολάπαρον ὑπόκενον, ἐπὶ τοσοῦτον δυσπνοήσουσιν, ἐφ’ ὅσον αὐτοῖς ἔχει κακῶς τὰ καθ’ ὑποχόνδριον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἡσυχῆ, πικρόχολος

And that the one described after her also himself breathed rapidly on account of the condition of the hypochondria, the passage itself will make clear, which runs in this manner: "The boy, who belonged to the woman, the sister of Apoemantus: hypochondria 954 large, and spleen, pneuma, evacuation bilious (πικρόχολος)." For just as he sometimes calls those who breathe rapidly "pneumatic" (πνευματώδεις), so at times he also writes simply "pneuma." And it is nothing to wonder at if, the hypochondria being large, the breathing became rapid. For indeed, going on further in his account of this patient, he will say again: "The hypochondria were tense, and moreover there was a slight dry little cough (βηχίον), quietly." For in general, as we said, whenever either a swelling or some pain forms in the parts about the hypochondria, they of necessity breathe small and rapid. So that it is nothing to wonder at either when, in what follows these, having first said "hypochondrium somewhat slack," he adds, "but not very pneumatic." For if indeed "slack" (λαπαρόν) means empty, and "somewhat slack" (ὑπολάπαρον) means somewhat empty, they will have difficulty of breathing to the same degree as the parts about the hypochondrium are in a bad state for them.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἡσυχῆ, πνεῦμα

And as for the patient written up after her, that he too breathed rapidly on account of the condition of the hypochondria, the text itself will make clear, which runs as follows: 'The boy who belonged to the woman, the sister of Apoimantus: hypochondria 954 large, and spleen; breath; evacuation bilious.' For just as he sometimes calls those who breathe rapidly 'full of pneuma' (pneumatōdeis), so too he sometimes writes simply 'breath' (pneuma). And it is no wonder that, the hypochondria being large, the breath became rapid. For further on he will again say of him: 'the hypochondria were distended, and there was also a slight dry cough, gently.' For in general, as we have said, whenever either a swelling or some pain arises in the parts at the hypochondria, they of necessity breathe small and rapid. So it is no wonder either when, in what follows, having first said 'hypochondrium somewhat soft', he adds 'but not very full of pneuma'. For if 'soft' (laparon) means empty and 'somewhat soft' (hypolaparon) somewhat empty, they will have difficulty in breathing to the same extent as the parts at the hypochondrium are in a bad state with them.

When, therefore, these parts are moderately affected, they will be moderately dyspnoeic; just as again, in the lower portions of the book, he writes somewhat as follows: "For the female house-slave, whom I saw when she was newly bought, 955 in whom the hardening (sklerysma) in the right parts was large, not very painful"—he reasonably adds, "and not very dyspnoeic." For if indeed the hardening was not very painful, it was likely that the woman was also not very dyspnoeic. So too, now at the end of the book, in giving his account of the older man, after first saying, "he wandered in his mind in an orderly manner," he added, "not very dyspnoeic." For the magnitude of the dyspnoea always comes about in accordance with the magnitude of the cause that produces the dyspnoea. But these things, too, have been written in the fourth book of the Epidemics, and they agree with what has been demonstrated by us concerning dyspnoea.

For the magnitude of the difficulty of breathing always corresponds to the magnitude of the cause producing the difficulty of breathing. In the fifth book of the Epidemics, in turn, the fourteenth patient is written up in the following way: 'In Larissa, Hipposthenes seemed to the physicians to be gripped by peripneumonia; but it was not so at all. The beginning: while wrestling he fell on his back on hard ground, and [his opponent] fell upon him; and he bathed in cold water, and remained so, and seemed to become heavier. On the following day he became feverish, and a rather dry cough took hold, and the 956 breathing was frequent.' Then, going on a little further, he says: 'whenever he coughed, he had pain in the chest and the back.' It is therefore not at all surprising that, with the parts in the thorax in such a condition, the breathing became frequent. And what is added at the end of the account, 'nor did he have stertor', is said as a demonstration that the man had not become peripneumonic, and it too agrees with what was said by us right at the beginning of this book, when we were explaining this passage in On Regimen in Acute Diseases: 'those who were suffocated by orthopnoea and stertor'. The thirty-ninth patient also is written up in the fifth book of the Epidemics, he too having frequent breathing. I shall quote here too the passage itself, which runs something like this: 'A child was struck by a mule in the belly and the liver; it died on the fourth day; it had frequent breathing, and was not in its senses, and fever held it.' It is not unclear in this case that the peritoneum and the diaphragm became inflamed together with the belly and the liver, and that for this reason the child also became delirious.

Again, in the fifth book of the Epidemics the fourteenth patient is written up in this way: 'In Larissa Hipposthenes seemed to the physicians to be gripped by peripneumonia, but it was not so at all. But it was not in accordance with the delirium 957 that he breathed with difficulty; for the greater condition, the one in the hypochondrium, by itself stamped the form of the respiration, while the lesser one was overcome. So much for what is in the fifth book. Passing again to the sixth, of which we have already earlier explained the passage it shares with the second, let us come to the remaining ones. And almost all of them are by now clear because of what has been said before. For when he writes 'breath' alone, simply in this way, in the concurrences of diseases, he has been shown to use it in place of the dyspnoea from which the breath becomes frequent; and this same thing, when it becomes frequent to a greater degree, it is his custom to call asthma. So that in the sixth book of the Epidemics, when he says 'breath is left behind within', we shall understand the dyspnoea accompanied by frequency. Likewise also in the case of the woman in Crannon, of whom he writes thus: 'spleen naturally large, burning fever, very red, breath' — the aforesaid dyspnoea is to be understood, in which the breath becomes frequent, as also in the case of the daughter of Agisis.

But it was not in accordance with the delirium 957 that he had difficulty of breathing; for the greater affection, the one in the hypochondrium, stamped the form of the respiration in accordance with itself, while the lesser was overcome. For it was shown that when, in the concurrences of symptoms of diseases, he writes simply 'breath' (pneuma) on its own in this way, he means the difficulty of breathing from which the breath becomes rapid; and this same thing, when it is made rapid to a still greater degree, it is his custom to call panting (asthma). And that frequent breath 958 differs from asthma he showed clearly. The passage runs as follows: the daughter of Agisis, while she was a girl, was of frequent breath; but having become a woman, being in distress not long after childbirth, she carelessly lifted a great weight, and immediately there seemed to be a noise in her chest; on the following day she had asthma and had pain in the right hip. Then, in what follows, she spat up frothy matter, he says, and at the beginning she was florid. And it is clear from the whole syndrome that, an old and hard-to-concoct phyma in the respiratory organs having burst, she spat up these things, and that it was on account of these phymata that she was of frequent breath. And again in the lower parts of the book, in the constitution which he began in this way, 'coughs began about the winter solstice', he says, 'in whom also the breath was raised up together with them' (and that the raised breath is the small and frequent one, we explained in what went before); indeed he also said that anginas occurred in that constitution.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπιπολαίως, ψοφῆσαι, ἀνθηρά, ξυνεμετεωρίζετό, ἀμέλει

That rapid breathing differs 958 from asthma he showed clearly. The passage runs thus: 'The daughter of Agisias, while still a girl, was rapid-breathing; having become a woman, and being in distress from a childbirth not long before, she lifted a great load carelessly, and at once she seemed to feel a crack in her chest; on the next day she had asthma and pain in the right hip.' Then next 'she spat up frothy matter', he says, 'and at the beginning she was florid.' And it is evident from the whole concurrence of symptoms that she spat these up when an old and hard-to-concoct tumour in the respiratory organs had ruptured, and that she was rapid-breathing because of these tumours. Again, in the later parts of the book, in the constitution which he began in this way: 'Coughs began about the winter solstice; and in those in whom the breathing was also raised up along with them', he says (and that 'raised' breathing is breathing that is small and rapid we have gone through in what precedes), he also said, of course, that synanchai occurred in that constitution.

Greek (Kühn)

μοχθηροὶ γοῦν οἱ τὸ μετέωρον ἀντὶ τοῦ μεγάλου νομίζοντες εἰρῆσθαι, διότι μετεωρίζει τὸν θώρακα, διαῖρον ἐπὶ πλεῖστον· 959 ὀλίγον δὲ προελθὼν, πνευματίας ὀνομάζει τινάς· καὶ εἴρηται καὶ περὶ τοῦδε πρόσθεν, ὡς ἤτοι τοὺς ἐμπεπνευματωμένους, ἢ τοὺς ἀσθματώδεις, οὕτως καλεῖ. τὸ δ’ ἐν τοῖς ἐφεξῆς λεγόμενον, ἄσημον πνεῦμα, σμικρόν ἐστι καὶ διὰ τοῦτ’ ἀφανὲς, οἷον καὶ ταῖς καλουμέναις ἀπνοίαις καὶ ταῖς ὑστερικαῖς πνιξὶ συνεδρεύει. τοιοῦτον δέ τι βούλεται δηλοῦν ἐξ αὐτῆς τῆς λέξεως ἐχούσης ὧδε· καὶ τὰ πνεύματα τοῖσι φθινώδεσι τὰ ἄσημα, κακὰ, καὶ τοῖσιν ἀτόκοισι· καὶ ὅσα ἄλλα τοιαῦτα ἀπὸ τῆς αὐτῆς καταστάσεως. αἱ γὰρ δὴ ἄτοκοι γυναῖκες, αὗται δὲ τοὐπίπαν εἰσὶν αἱ μὴ καθαιρόμεναι καλῶς, ἁλίσκονται μάλιστα ταῖς ἀπνοίαις, ὥσπερ ἀμέλει καὶ ὅσαι χηρεύουσιν ἐπὶ πλεῖστον. ὥσθ’ ὅσα μὴ διάσημα σαφῶς πνεύματα, μηδ’ ἐστὶν ἡμῖν κατάδηλα, ταῦτ’ ἄσημα κέκληκεν. ἐκ τούτων ὁρμώμενος ἄν τις εὑρίσκοι καὶ χωρὶς ἐμοῦ τἄλλα πάντα τὰ περὶ δυσπνοίας εἰρημένα κατὰ τε τὰ γνήσια καὶ τὰ νόθα τῶν βιβλίων, καίτοι σχεδὸν ἡμεῖς πάντα διήλθομεν ἐν τῷδε τῷ λόγῳ τὰ γνήσια.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πνευματίας, ἐμπεπνευματωμένους

At any rate, those are mistaken who suppose that 'elevated' (μετέωρον) is said in place of 'great', on the ground that it lifts up the thorax, raising it to the greatest extent. 959 A little further on he names certain persons pneumatiai (πνευματίας); and it has been said about this too earlier, that he so calls either those who are filled with wind or those who are asthmatic. The 'unmarked breath' (ἄσημον πνεῦμα) spoken of in what follows is small, and for this reason imperceptible, such as also accompanies the so-called apnoeas and the hysterical suffocations. That he wishes to indicate something of this sort is clear from the wording itself, which runs thus: 'and in the consumptive, the unmarked breaths are bad, and in childless women; and whatever other such things arise from the same constitution.' For childless women—and these for the most part are those who are not well purged—are especially seized by apnoeas, just as, of course, are those who remain widowed for a very long time. So that whatever breaths are not clearly marked, nor manifest to us, these he has called 'unmarked'. Starting from these, one could discover even without me all the other things said about difficulty of breathing in both the genuine and the spurious books, although we have gone through nearly all the genuine ones in this treatise.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πνευματίας, ἄσημον, ἀτόκοισι, μὴ καθαιρόμεναι καλῶς

Wrong, then, are those who think that 'elevated' has been said in place of 'large', on the ground that it elevates the thorax, distending it to the utmost. 959 A little further on he names certain people pneumatiai; this too has been discussed earlier, namely that he so calls either those who are inflated with pneuma or those who are asthmatic. What is said in what follows, 'indistinct breathing', is small and for that reason imperceptible, such as also attends the so-called apnoeas and uterine suffocations. He means to indicate something of this kind, as appears from the wording itself, which runs thus: 'And indistinct breathings in the consumptive are bad, and in the childless; and all other such things from the same constitution.' For childless women (and these are for the most part those who are not properly purged) are most of all seized by apnoeas, as indeed are also those who remain widowed for a very long time. So the breathings that are not clearly distinct and are not plainly evident to us, these he has called indistinct. Setting out from these, one could find even without me all the other things said about difficulty of breathing in both the genuine and the spurious books, although we have gone through nearly all the genuine ones in this treatise.

Greek (Kühn)

ἀλλ’ ἐπεὶ καὶ τὰ Θεσσαλοῦ τοῦ υἱέος αὐτοῦ καὶ 960 τὰ Πολύβου τοῦ γαμβροῦ, τῆς Ἱπποκράτους ἐστὶ τέχνης, οὐ πόῤῥω δ’ αὐτῶν οὐδ’ ὅσα δοκεῖ μὲν Εὐρυφῶντος εἶναι, φέρεται δ’ ἐν τοῖς Ἱπποκράτους, ἐάν μοι σχολὴ γένηταί ποτε πλείων, ἄλλο τέταρτον προσθήσω βιβλίον, ἐξηγούμενον ἃ κατὰ τὰ λοιπὰ τῶν ἐπιγεγραμμένων Ἱπποκράτους εἴρηται περὶ δυσπνοίας. νυνὶ δ’ ἑτέρων κατεπειγόντων, οὐκ ἐπ’ ἐκεῖνα μεταβήσομαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But since the writings of Thessalus his son and 960 those of Polybus his son-in-law also belong to the art of Hippocrates, and not far from them are also those which seem to be by Euryphon but are transmitted among the works of Hippocrates, if ever I come to have more leisure, I shall add another, fourth book, expounding what is said about dyspnoea in the remaining works inscribed with the name of Hippocrates. But for now, since other matters are pressing, I shall not pass over to those.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

But since the writings of Thessalus his son and 960 those of Polybus his son-in-law also belong to the art of Hippocrates, and not far from them are those which are thought to be by Euryphon but are transmitted among the works of Hippocrates, if ever I should come to have more leisure, I shall add another, fourth book, explaining what has been said about difficulty of breathing in the remaining writings that bear the title of Hippocrates. But now, since other matters are pressing, I shall not pass on to those.
